Backward-forward characterization of attainable set for conservation laws with spatially discontinuous flux Thanks: Dipartimento di Matematica “Tullio Levi-Civita”, Università di Padova, Italy, E-mail: ancona@math.unipd.it, luca.talamini@math.unipd.it
Fabio Ancona and Luca Talamini

Abstract
. Consider a scalar conservation law with a spatially discontinuous flux at a single point x = 0 x=0 , and assume that the flux is uniformly convex when x ≠ 0 x\neq 0 . Given an interface connection ( A , B ) (A,B) , we define a backward solution operator consistent with the concept of A ​ B AB -entropy solution [ 4 , 16 , 19 ] . We then analyze the family 𝒜 [ A ​ B ] ​ ( T ) \mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T) of profiles that can be attained at time T > 0 T>0 by A ​ B AB -entropy solutions with 𝐋 ∞ {\bf L^{\infty}} -initial data. We provide a characterization of 𝒜 [ A ​ B ] ​ ( T ) \mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T) as fixed points of the backward-forward solution operator. As an intermediate step we establish for the first time a full characterization of 𝒜 [ A ​ B ] ​ ( T ) \mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T) in terms of unilateral constraints and Oleǐnik-type estimates, valid for all connections. Building on such a characterization we derive uniform B ​ V BV bounds on the flux of A ​ B AB -entropy solutions, which in turn yield the 𝐋 loc 𝟏 \bf L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}} -Lipschitz continuity in time of these solutions.



1.
Introduction

Consider the initial value problem for the scalar conservation law in one space dimension

(1.1)

\displaystyle u_{t}+f(x,u)_{x}=0,\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\quad t\geq 0,

(1.2)

\displaystyle u(x,0)=u_{0}(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},

where
u=u(x,t)
is the state variable and the flux
f
is a space discontinuous function given by

(1.3)

f(x,u)=\begin{cases}f_{l}(u),\qquad x<0,\\
f_{r}(u),\qquad x>0\,.\end{cases}

We assume that
f_{l},f_{r}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

are twice continuously differentiable, uniformly convex maps that satisfy

(1.4)

f_{l}^{\prime\prime}(u),\ f_{r}^{\prime\prime}(u)\geq a>0\,.

Conservation
laws with discontinuous flux
serve as mathematical models
for:
oil reservoir simulation
[
30
,
31
]
;
traffic flow dynamics
with roads of varying amplitudes or surface conditions
[
38
]
;
radar shape-from-shading problems
[
39
]
;
blood flow in endovascular treatments
[
28
,
20
]
;
and for many other different applications
(see
[
6
]
and references therein).

We recall that problems of this type
do not posses classical solutions globally defined in time (even in the continuous flux case when
f_{l}=f_{r}
), since, regardless of how smooth the initial data are, they can develop discontinuities (shocks) in finite time
because of the nonlinearity of the equation. To achieve existence results, one has to look for
weak distributional
solution
that, for sake of uniqueness, satisfy
the classical Kružkov entropy inequalities
away from the point of flux discontinuity, and a further interface entropy condition
at the flux-discontinuity interface
x=0
.

Various type
of interface-entropy conditions have
been introduced in the literature
according with the different physical phenomena modelled by (
1.1
) (see
[
11
,
12
]
).
Here,
as in
[
6
]
,
for modellization and control treatment reasons
we employ an admissibility criterion involving the so-called
interface connection

(A,B)
,
which yields the Definition
2.2
of
AB
-entropy solution

(cfr.
[
4
,
19
]
).
A connection
(A,B)
is a pair of states connected by a stationary weak solution
of (
1.1
),
taking values
A
for
x<0
, and
B
for
x>0
, which has characteristics diverging
from (or parallel to) the flux-discontinuity interface
x=0

(see Definition
2.1
).
The admissibility criterion
for an
AB
-entropy solution

can be equivalently formulated in terms of an interface entropy condition
or of Kružkov-type entropy inequalities adapted to the particular connection
(A,B)
taken into account
(cfr.
[
4
,
16
,
19
]
). Relying on
these
extended entropy inequalities
and using an adapted version of the Kružkov doubling of variables argument, one can establish
{\bf L^{1}}
-stability and uniqueness of
AB
-entropy solutions to the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)-(
1.2
)
(see
[
19
,
29
]
). We shall adopt the semigroup notation

u(x,t)\doteq\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}u_{0}(x)
for the unique solution of (
1.1
)-(
1.2
).

In this paper we are concerned as in
[
2
,
6
]

with a controllability problem
for (
1.1
)
where one regards the initial data as controls and study
the corresponding
attainable set

at a fixed time
T>0
:

(1.5)

\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)\doteq\big\{\mathcal{{\mathcal{S}}_{\it T}^{\,[{\it AB}\,]+}}u_{0}\;:\;u_{0}\in{\bf L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\big\}\,.

In the same spirit of
[
26
,
27
,
32
,
36
]

we introduce a
backward solution operator

(see Definition
2.16
)

(1.6)

\mathcal{S}_{T}^{\,[AB]-}:{\bf L^{\infty}}(\mathbb{R})\to{\bf L^{\infty}}(\mathbb{R}),\qquad\quad\omega\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{T}^{\,[AB]-}\omega\,,

and we
characterize the attainable targets for (
1.1
) at a time horizon
T>0

as fixed-points of the composition
backward-forward operator

{\mathcal{S}}_{\it T}^{\,[{\it AB}\,]+}\circ\mathcal{S}_{T}^{\,[AB]-}
, as stated in our first main result:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
f
be a flux as in (
1.3
)
satisfying the assumption (
1.4
),
and let

(A,B)

be a connection. Then,
for every
T>0
,
and for any
\omega\in{\bf L^{\infty}}(\mathbb{R})
,
the following conditions are equivalent.

(1)

\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)
,

(2)

\omega=\mathcal{{\mathcal{S}}_{\it T}^{\,[{\it AB}\,]+}}\circ\mathcal{S}_{T}^{\,[AB]-}\omega\,.

Moreover, if
(A,B)
is a non critical connection, i.e. if
A\neq\theta_{l},B\neq\theta_{r}
, then the condition (2) is equivalent to

(1)’

\omega\in\mathcal{A}_{bv}^{[AB]}(T)
, where

(1.7)

\mathcal{A}_{bv}^{[AB]}(T)\doteq\big\{\mathcal{{\mathcal{S}}_{\it T}^{\,[{\it AB}\,]+}}u_{0}\;:\;u_{0}\in BV_{loc}(\mathbb{R})\big\}\,,

and it holds true

(1.8)

\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)=\mathcal{A}_{bv}^{[AB]}(T)\,.

Clearly the main content of Theorem
1.1
are the implication (1)
\Longrightarrow
(2)
and (1)’
\Longrightarrow
(2),
since the reverse implications
are straightforward once we define the backward operator

\mathcal{S}_{T}^{\,[AB]-}
and verify that, in the case
of a non critical connection, one has
\mathcal{S}_{T}^{\,[AB]-}u_{0}\in BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})

for all
u_{0}\in{\bf L^{\infty}}(\mathbb{R})
.
This last property is an immediate consequence of
the uniform BV bounds
on
AB
-entropy solutions established in Proposition
6.1
, since the backward operator
\mathcal{S}_{T}^{\,[AB]-}
is defined in terms of the forward operator
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{t}^{\,[\overline{B}\,\overline{A}\,]+}
(see Definition
2.16
).

The proof of (1)
\Longrightarrow
(2)
and (1)’
\Longrightarrow
(2)
are obtained in two steps:

(I)

First, we show that any attainable profile
\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)

belongs
to a class of functions

\mathscr{A}\subset BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})

which satisfy suitable Oleǐnik-type inequalities and pointwise constraints
related to the
(A,B)
-connection
in intervals containing the origin
(see Theorem
4.3
,

4.9
,
4.11
,
4.14
).

We classify the different type of
pointwise constraints satisfied by the attainable profiles in
\mathscr{A}
highlighting the ones that can be recovered as limiting
cases (see Remarks
4.6
,
4.10
,
4.13
,
4.15
,

4.16
).

(II)

Next, we prove that any element of

\mathscr{A}
is a fixed point of
the composition backward-forward operator
{\mathcal{S}}_{\it T}^{\,[{\it AB}\,]+}\circ\mathcal{S}_{T}^{\,[AB]-}
.
Namely, for any given
\omega\in\mathscr{A}

we construct an initial datum
u_{0}\in{\bf L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}
such that
\omega={\mathcal{S}}_{\it T}^{\,[{\it AB}\,]+}u_{0}
, and then we show that indeed
u_{0}=\mathcal{S}_{T}^{\,[AB]-}\omega
.

These two steps are firstly carried out
in the case of a non critical
connection
(A,B)

and of attainable profiles

\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)\cap BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
.
The proofs are obtained
exploiting as in
[
6
]
the theory of generalized characteristics
by Dafermos
[
24
]
,
applied to the setting of discontinuous flux, and relying on the duality property of the backward and forward solution operators. Next, we address the case of a critical connection and
of attainable profiles
\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)
relying on the
\bf L^{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{loc}}}
-stability of the map

(A,B,u_{0})\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}u_{0}

(see Theorem
2.8
).

Some remarks are here in order.

•

The results of Theorem
1.1
extend to the present setting of space discontinuous fluxes the similar
characterization of attainable profiles in terms of the backward solution operator
obtained in
[
23
, Theorem 3.1, Corollary 3.2]
and
[
32
, Corollary 1]

for conservation laws with strictly convex flux independent on the space variable.

•

The characterization of
\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)

obtained in this paper unveils the presence of
two classes
of attainable states
for
critical and non critical
connections

that were
not detected in
[
2
,
6
]
, see Remarks
4.7
,
4.18
.

•

The characterization of
attainable profiles for (
1.1
),
(
1.3
) in terms of
unilateral constraints and Oleǐnik-type estimates provides a powerful tool to investigate regularity properties
of the solutions to (
1.1
),
(
1.3
). In particular,
we build on such a characterization to
derive
uniform BV bounds

on
AB
-entropy solutions
with initial datum in
{\bf L^{\infty}}
(in the case of
non critical connections), and
on the flux
of
AB
-entropy solutions
(for general connections).
This is a fairly non-trivial result since it is well known
[
1
]
that the total variation of
AB
-entropy solutions
may well blow up in a neighborhood of the
flux-discontinuity interface
x=0
.
Thanks to these uniform BV bounds,
we can then establish the

\bf L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-Lipschitz continuity
in time of
AB
-entropy solutions
.

•

The proof that Theorem
1.1

holds for critical connections once we know that Theorem
1.1

is verified by non critical connections
relies on a perturbation argument
for attainable profiles.
This construction yields an
approximate controllability
result since it
provides a general explicit procedure to approximate an attainable profile for a critical connection by attainable profiles for non critical connections.

Note furthermore that, by the
backward non-uniqueness
of (
1.1
)
(due to the possible presence of shocks
in its solutions),
there may exist in general multiple initial data
u_{0}

that are
steered
by (
1.1
) to

\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)
.
In fact, an important control problem related to the one considered in this paper is the inverse design,
which has the goal to reconstruct
the set of initial data
u_{0}
evolving to a given
attainable target
\omega

(see
[
23
,
32
,
36
,
37
]

for conservation laws with convex flux independent on the space variable, and
[
27
]

for Hamilton-Jacobi equations with convex Hamiltonian).
On the other hand, when
a target state
\omega
is not attainable at time
T>0
, the image of
\omega

through the backward-forward operator
{\mathcal{S}}_{\it T}^{\,[{\it AB}\,]+}\circ\mathcal{S}_{T}^{\,[AB]-}
represents a natural candidate to construct a reachable function which is “as
close as possible” (in an appropriate sense) to the observed state
\omega
(see
[
26
]

in the case of Hamilton-Jacobi
and Burgers equations).

The results of the present paper
provide a key building block
to address both of these problems, namely
the characterization of the aforementioned set of initial data leading to a given
attainable target
\omega

for (
1.1
),
and the analysis of the properties
of the backward-forward operator
{\mathcal{S}}_{\it T}^{\,[{\it AB}\,]+}\circ\mathcal{S}_{T}^{\,[AB]-}

related to optimization problems for unattainable target profiles, which are
pursued in the
forthcoming paper
[
10
]
.

In the case
of non-convex flux, an explicit characterizations of the attainable set in terms of
Oleǐnik-type estimates seems difficult to obtain and only partial results are present in the literature, see for example
[
14
]
.
For systems of conservation laws, the problem has been considered in
[
13
]
(triangular systems) and in
[
22
]
(chromatography system).
For a characterization of the attainable set in terms of fixed points of a backward-forward operator,
a key point would be to
provide a proper
definition of backward operator
in these more general contexts,
which is lacking at the moment, making also the analysis of the inverse design problem nontrivial.

The paper is organized as follows. In §
2

we recall the definitions of interface connection
(A,B)
, of
AB
-entropy solution and of
AB
-backward solution operator.
We also collect the stability properties
of the
{\bf L^{1}}
-contractive semigroup of
AB
-entropy solutions.
In §
3

we establish the duality property
of the backward and forward solution operators, which constitutes a fundamental ingredient
of the proof of Theorem
1.1
.
§
4

collects the precise statements of the
results on the characterization
of the attainable set
\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)

via
Oleǐnik-type inequalities and state constraints. We also include
the statement of Theorem
4.17

which contains the equivalence of
conditions (1), (2) of
Theorem
1.1

with the characterization of

\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)
in terms of
Oleǐnik-type inequalities and unilateral constraints.
In §
5

we carry out the rather technical and involved proof of Theorem
4.17
.
At the beginning of the section,
for reader’s convenience,
we provide a roadmap of the proof of Theorem
4.17
,
where we also highlight the key innovative parts of the paper.
In §
6

we derive uniform BV bounds
on
AB
-entropy solutions
in the case of non critical connections,
and on the flux of
AB
-entropy solutions
for general connections.
In Appendix
A

we establish the
{\bf L^{1}}
-stability
properties of the semigroup of
AB
-entropy solutions with respect to time and with respect to the connections.
In Appendix
B

we provide, for sake of completeness, a simple proof
of the non existence of rarefactions
emanating from the interface
x=0
,
which is a distinctive feature of

AB
-entropy solutions.
Finally, in Appendix
C

we derive some lower/upper
{\bf L^{1}}
-semicontinuity property for solutions to conservation laws, used to recover the
proof of Theorem
4.17

in the case of critical connections
once we know the validity of Theorem
4.17

for non critical connections.

2.
Basic definitions and general setting

2.1.
Connections and
AB
-entropy solutions

We recall here the definitions and properties of interface connection and of entropy admissible solution introduced in
[
4
]
.

Definition 2.1

(
Interface Connection).

Let
f
be a flux as in (
1.3
)
satisfying the assumption (
1.4
),
and let
\theta_{l}
,
\theta_{r}
denote the unique critical points
of
f_{l},f_{r}
, respectively.
A pair of values
(A,B)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
is called a
connection
if

(1)

f_{l}(A)=f_{r}(B)
,

(2)

A\leq\theta_{l}
and
B\geq\theta_{r}
.

We will say that connection
(A,B)
is
critical
if
A=\theta_{l}

or
B=\theta_{r}
.

u
f_{l}
f_{r}
A
B
\theta_{l}
\theta_{r}

Figure 1
.
An example of connection
(A,B)
with
f_{l},f_{r}
strictly convex fluxes

A
B
x
t

Figure 2
.
The stationary undercompressive solution
c^{AB}
.

Observe that condition (2) is equivalent
to:
f^{\prime}_{l}(A)\leq 0
,
f^{\prime}_{r}(B)\geq 0
.
Therefore, if
(A,B)
is a connection,
then
the function

(2.1)

c^{AB}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{=}}\begin{cases}A,&x\leq 0,\\
B,&x\geq 0\end{cases}

is a weak stationary undercompressive (or marginally undercompressive) solution of (
1.1
), since the characteristics diverge from, or are parallel to, the flux-discontinuity interface (see Figure
2
).
In relation to the function
c^{AB}
the
adapted entropy

\eta_{AB}(x,u)=\big|u-c^{AB}(x)\big|
is introduced in
[
19
]
. Then, in the spirit of
[
17
]
, the entropy
\eta_{AB}
is employed in
[
19
]
to select a unique
solution of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) that satisfies the interface entropy inequality

(2.2)

\left|u-c^{AB}\right|_{t}+\left[\mathrm{sgn}(u-c^{AB})(f(x,u)-f(x,c^{AB}))\right]_{x}\leq 0,\quad\textrm{in}\ \ \mathcal{D}^{\prime}\,,

in the sense of distributions, which leads to the following definition.

Definition 2.2

(
AB
-entropy solution).

Let
(A,B)
be a connection
and let
c^{AB}
be the function defined in (
2.1
). A function
u\in\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,+\infty[\,){~\cap~\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,+\infty),\;\mathbf{L}^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}))}
is said to be an
AB
-entropy solution
of the problem (
1.1
),(
1.2
) if the following holds:

(1)

u
is a distributional solution of (
1.1
) on
\mathbb{R}\times\,]0,+\infty[
, that is, for all test functions
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}

with compact support
contained in
\mathbb{R}\times\,]0,+\infty[
,
it holds true

(2.3)

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big\{u\phi_{t}+f(x,u)\phi_{x}\big\}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t=0\,.

(2)

u
is a Kružkov entropy weak solution of (
1.1
),(
1.2
) on
(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})\times\,]0,+\infty[
, that is
the initial condition (
1.2
) is satisfied almost everywhere,
and:

(2.a)

for any non-negative test function
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}
with compact support
contained in
]-\infty,0[\,\times

\,]0,+\infty[
, it holds true

(2.4)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\big\{|u-k|\phi_{t}+\mathrm{sgn}(u-k)\left(f_{l}(u)-f_{l}(k)\right)\phi_{x}\big\}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\geq 0,\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{R}\,;

(2.b)

for any non-negative test function
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}
with compact support
contained in
]0,+\infty[\,\times

]0,+\infty[
, it holds true

(2.5)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big\{|u-k|\phi_{t}+\mathrm{sgn}(u-k)\left(f_{r}(u)-f_{r}(k)\right)\phi_{x}\big\}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\geq 0,\quad\forall k\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(3)

u
satisfies a Kružkov-type entropy inequality relative to the connection
(A,B)
, that is,
for any non-negative test function
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}
with compact support
contained in
\mathbb{R}\times]0,+\infty[
, it holds true

(2.6)

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big\{\left|u-c^{AB}\right|\phi_{t}+\mathrm{sgn}(u-c^{AB})\left(f(x,u)-f(x,c^{AB})\right)\phi_{x}\big\}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\geq 0\,.

Remark 2.3
.

Since
an
AB
-entropy solution
u
is in particular an entropy weak solution
of a scalar conservation law
with uniformly convex flux,
on
]-\infty,0[\,\times\,]0,+\infty[
,
and on
]0,+\infty[\,\times\,]0,+\infty[

(by property (2) of Definition
2.2

and assumption (
1.4
)),
it follows that
u(\cdot\,,t)\in{BV}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})
for any
t>0.

Here
BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\})

denotes the set of functions that have finite total variation on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
.
On the other hand,
relying on a result in
[
40
]

(see also
[
41
]
),
we deduce that

u
admits left and right strong traces at
x=0
for a.e.
t>0
, i.e. that
there exist the one-sided limits

(2.7)

u_{l}(t)\doteq u(0-,t),\qquad u_{r}(t)\doteq u(0+,t),\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$}\,.

We point out that a consequence of
the characterizatin of attainable profiles
provided by our results (Theorems
4.3
,
4.9
,
4.11
,
4.14
)
will be that these limits are actually defined at every time
t>0
(not only at almost every time).
Moreover,
since
u
is also a distributional solution of (
1.1
) on
\mathbb{R}\times\,]0,+\infty[

(by property (1) of Definition
2.2
), we deduce that

u
must satisfy the Rankine-Hugoniot condition at the interface
x=0:

(2.8)

f_{l}(u_{l}(t))=f_{r}(u_{r}(t)),\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$}.

In (
2.7
) and throughout the paper, for the one-sided limits of a function
u(x)
we use the notation

(2.9)

u(x\,\pm)\doteq\lim_{y\to x\,\pm}u(y).

In relation to a connection
(A,B)

consider the function

(2.10)

I^{AB}(u_{l},u_{r})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cdot}}{{=}}\mathrm{sgn}(u_{r}-B)\left(f_{r}(u_{r})-f_{r}(B)\right)\\
-\mathrm{sgn}(u_{l}-A)\left(f_{l}(u_{l})-f_{l}(A)\right),\qquad u_{l},u_{r}\in\mathbb{R}\,,

which is useful to characterize
the interface entropy admissibility criterion. In fact, by the analysis in
[
19
, Lemma 3.2]
and
[
16
, Section 4.8]
,
it follows that, because of
condition (1) of Definition
2.2

and assumption (
1.4
),
the following holds.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
u\in\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,+\infty))

be a function satisfying conditions (1)-(2) of Definition
2.2
.
Then, condition (3) is equivalent to the

AB
interface entropy condition

(3)’

(2.11)

I^{AB}(u_{l}(t),u_{r}(t))\leq 0\qquad\quad\text{for a.e. $t>0$\,.}

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
(A,B)
be a connection. Then, for any pair
(u_{l},u_{r})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the conditions

(2.12)

f_{l}(u_{l})=f_{r}(u_{r}),\qquad\quad I^{AB}(u_{l},u_{r})\leq 0\,,

are equivalent to the conditions

(2.13)

\displaystyle f_{l}(u_{l})=f_{r}(u_{r})\geq f_{l}(A)=f_{r}(B),

\displaystyle\big(u_{l}\leq\theta_{l},\quad u_{r}\geq\theta_{r}\big)\ \ \Longrightarrow\ \ u_{l}=A,\ \ u_{r}=B\,.

The first condition in (
2.13
)
tells us that, when we choose
a connection
(A,B)

and we employ the concept of

AB
-entropy solution, we are
imposing a constraint (from below) on the flux at the interface
x=0
.
In order to achieve existence, we need to compensate for this constraint with an additional freedom in the admissibility criteria.
In fact, the second condition in (
2.13
)
prescribes the admissibility of exactly one undercompressive wave at the interface, given by
c^{AB}
in (
2.1
).
This rule corresponds to the

(A,B)
characteristic condition
in
[
19
, Definition 1.4]
.

Remark 2.6
.

In view of (
2.8
),
we can extend the classical concept of genuine characteristic for solutions to conservation laws with continuous fluxes
(see
[
24
]
)
by considering also characteristics that are refracted by the discontinuity interface
x=0
. Thus, we will say that a polygonal line
\vartheta:[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
is a
genuine characteristic for an
AB
-entropy solution
u
if one of the following cases occurs:

(i)

\vartheta(t)<0
for all
t\in\,]0,T[\,
, and
\vartheta
is a characteristic for the restriction of
u
on
]-\infty,0[\,\times\,]0,T[\,
;

(ii)

\vartheta(t)>0
for all
t\in\,]0,T[\,
, and
\vartheta
is a characteristic for the restriction of
u
on
]0,+\infty[\,\times\,]0,T[\,
;

(iii)

there exists
\tau\in\,]0,T[
, such that:

-

\vartheta(t)<0
for all
t\in\,]0,\tau[\,
, and
\vartheta
is a characteristic for the restriction of
u
on
]-\infty,0[\,\times\,]0,\tau[\,
,

-

\vartheta(t)>0
for all
t\in\,]\tau,T[\,
, and
\vartheta
is a characteristic for the restriction of
u
on
]0,+\infty[\,\times\,]\tau,T[\,
,

or viceversa.

-

f_{l}(u_{l}(\tau))=f_{r}(u_{r}(\tau))
and
I^{AB}(u_{l}(\tau),u_{r}(\tau))\leq 0
,

where we are using the term “characteristic” for a classical genuine characteristic of a solution to the conservation law
u_{t}+f_{l}(u)_{x}=0

on
\{x<0\}
, or to the conservation law
u_{t}+f_{r}(u)_{x}=0

on
\{x>0\}
.

Remark 2.7

(Local solutions)
.

Throughout the paper we say that
a function
u\in\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is
a (local)
AB
-entropy solution
of (
1.1
)
on a domain

\Omega\doteq\big\{(t,x)\;|\;t\in[a,b],\quad\gamma_{1}(t)<x<\gamma_{2}(t)\big\}\subset\mathbb{R}\times\,]0,+\infty[

where
\gamma_{1}<\gamma_{2}:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
are Lipschitz curves if it satisfies the conditions of Definition
2.2
localized on
\Omega
.
Namely, if the following holds:

(1)

For any test functions
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}(\Omega)

with compact support
contained in
\Omega
,
it holds true (
2.3
).

(2)

The map
t\mapsto u(\cdot,t)
is continuous from
I\doteq\{t>0\,:\,(x,t)\in\Omega\}
to
{\bf L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}}(\Omega_{t})
,

\Omega_{t}\doteq\{x\,:\,(x,t)\in\Omega\}
,
and it holds:

(2.a)

for any non-negative test function
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}(\Omega)
with compact support
contained in
\Omega\cap\big(]-\infty,0[\,\times\,]0,+\infty[\big)
, it holds true (
2.4
);

(2.b)

or any non-negative test function
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}(\Omega)
with compact support
contained in
\Omega\cap\big(]0,+\infty[\,\times\,]0,+\infty[\big)
, it holds true (
2.5
).

(3)

For any test functions
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}(\Omega)

with compact support
contained in
\Omega
,
it holds true (
2.6
).

We will sometimes implicitly use the following fact: assume that two local
AB
-entropy solutions
u_{1},u_{2}
, of (
1.1
)
are given on two
disjoint domains
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}
,
such that
\partial\Omega_{1}\cap\partial\Omega_{2}=\Gamma
,
where
\Gamma
is the graph of a Lipschitz curve
\gamma:[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
, with
\Omega_{1}\subset\{x\leq\gamma(t)\}
,
\Omega_{2}\subset\{x\geq\gamma(t)\}
.
Moreover, assume that

u_{1}(t,\gamma(t)-)=u_{2}(t,\gamma(t)+)
for a.e.
t\in[a,b]
such that
\gamma(t)\neq 0
, and that

f_{l}(u_{1}(0-,t))=f_{r}(u_{2}(0+,t)),\qquad\quad I^{AB}(u_{1}(0-,t),u_{2}(0+,t))\leq 0,

for a.e.
t\in[a,b]
such that
\gamma(t)=0
.
Then, by standard arguments one can deduce that the function

u(x,t)=\begin{cases}u_{1}(x,t)&\text{if $(x,t)\in\Omega_{1}$},\\
u_{2}(x,t)&\text{if $(x,t)\in\Omega_{2}$}\end{cases}

is an
AB
-entropy solution of (
1.1
) on
\Omega
.

It was proved in
[
4
,
19
]
(see also
[
16
,
29
]
)
that
AB
-entropy solutions of (
1.1
),(
1.2
)
with bounded initial data are unique and form an
\mathbf{L}^{1}
-contractive semigroup. Moreover, we will show that they are
{\bf L^{1}}
-stable also with respect to the
values
A,B
of the connection.
This type of stability, beside
being used to extend our main results
from the case of non critical connections to the critical one,
has
an interest on its own.
We will also
prove that
AB
-entropy solutions of (
1.1
),(
1.2
)
are
{\bf L^{1}}
-Lipschitz continuous in time. This property is an immediate consequence of the BV regularity
of such solutions in the case of
non critical connections.
Instead,
in the case of critical connections where

A=\theta_{l}
or
B=\theta_{r}
, and
f_{l}(\theta_{l})\neq f_{r}(\theta_{r})
, the total variation
of an
AB
-entropy solution
may well blow up in a neighborhood of the
flux-discontinuity interface
x=0
,
as shown in
[
1
]
.
However, we recover the
{\bf L^{1}}
-Lipschitz continuity in time also in this case exploiting the BV regularity of the flux of an
AB
-entropy solution, which
is established relying on the analysis pursued in this paper.
We collect all these (old and new) results in the following statement:

Theorem 2.8
.

(Semigroup of
AB
-Entropy Solutions)

Let
f
be a flux as in (
1.3
)
satisfying the assumption (
1.4
),
and let

(A,B)

be a connection. Then there exists a map

(2.14)

\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]+}:[0,+\infty[\,\times\,{\bf L^{\infty}}(\mathbb{R})\to{\bf L^{\infty}}(\mathbb{R}),\qquad\quad(t,u_{0})\mapsto\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}u_{0}\,,

enjoying the following properties:

(i)

For each
u_{0}\in{\bf L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, the function
u(x,t)\doteq\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}u_{0}(x)
provides the unique bounded,
AB
-entropy solution of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
), (
1.2
).

(ii)

\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]+}_{0}u_{0}=u_{0}
,
\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]+}_{s}\circ\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}=\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]+}_{s+t}u_{0}
, for all
t,s\geq 0
,
u_{0}\in{\bf L^{\infty}}(\mathbb{R})
.

(iii)

For any
u_{0},v_{0}\in{\bf L^{\infty}}(\mathbb{R})
, there exists a constant
L>0
,
depending on
f
and
on
\|u_{0}\|_{\bf L^{\infty}},\|v_{0}\|_{\bf L^{\infty}}
,
such that, for any
R>0
, it holds:

\big\|\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}u_{0}-\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}v_{0}\big\|_{{\bf L}^{1}([-R,R])}\leq\big\|u_{0}-v_{0}\big\|_{{\bf L}^{1}([-R-Lt,R+Lt])}
,
for all
t\geq 0
.

(iv)

For any
u_{0}\in{\bf L^{\infty}}(\mathbb{R})
,
and for any
R>0
, it holds:

\big\|\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}u_{0}-\mathcal{S}_{t}^{[A^{\prime}B^{\prime}]+}u_{0}\big\|_{{\bf L}^{1}([-R,R])}\leq 2\,t\,\big|f_{r}(B)-f_{r}(B^{\prime})\big|
,

for all connections
(A,B)
,
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, and for all
t\geq 0
.

(v)

For any

u_{0}\in{\bf L^{\infty}}(\mathbb{R})
,
and for any
R>0
,
there exists a constant
C_{R}>0

depending on
f
,
\|u_{0}\|_{\bf L^{1}}
,
R
,
and on the connection
(A,B)
,
such that it holds:

\big\|\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}u_{0}-\mathcal{S}^{[AB]+}_{s}u_{0}\big\|_{{\bf L}^{1}{([-R,R])}}\leq\dfrac{C_{R}}{t}|s-t|
, for all
s>t>0
.

The proof of the new properties (iv)-(v) is given in Appendix
A
.

Remark 2.9
.

Most of the literature on conservation laws with discontinuous flux as in (
1.3
)
considers fluxes
f_{l},f_{r}
satisfying

(2.15)

f_{l}(0)=f_{r}(0),\quad f_{l}(1)=f_{r}(1)\,,\qquad\quad\theta_{l}\geq 0,\quad\theta_{r}\leq 1.

However
the existence of an
\mathbf{L}^{1}
-contractive semigroup of
AB
-entropy solutions of the Cauchy problem (
1.1
), (
1.2
),
remains valid also without this assumption as shown for example in
[
29
]
.
On the other hand,
by a reparametrization of the fluxes, one can always
reduce the problem to the setting where
the critical points
of
f_{l},f_{r}
satisfy (
2.15
).
In fact, given
f_{l},f_{r}
,
for any pair of invertible affine maps
\phi_{l},\phi_{r}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, we can observe that a map
u(x,t)

is an
AB
-entropy solution
of (
1.1
), (
1.3
) with fluxes
f_{l},f_{r}
if and only if

\widetilde{u}(x,t)\doteq\begin{cases}\phi_{l}^{-1}(u)&\text{if $x<0,$}\\
\phi_{r}^{-1}(u)&\text{if $x>0,$}\end{cases}

is an
AB
-entropy solution of (
1.1
), (
1.3
) with fluxes
f_{l}\circ\phi_{l},\,f_{r}\circ\phi_{r}
.

Remark 2.10
.

By the analysis in
[
29
, §3.1]

(see also
[
6
, Remark 4.1]
)
it follows that, for every
M>0
,
there exists
C_{M}>0
such that, if
\|u_{0}\|_{{\bf L}^{\infty}}\leq M
, and
A,B\leq M
, then

\|\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}u_{0}\|_{{\bf L}^{\infty}}\leq C_{M}
, for all
t>0
.

Corollary 2.11
.

Let
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n}
be a sequence of connections that converges in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to a connection
(A,B)
, and let
\{u_{n,0}\}_{n}

be a sequence of functions in
{\bf L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})

that converges in
\bf L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
to
u_{0}\in{\bf L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.
Let
u_{n,l},u_{n,r}
denote, respectively,
the left and right traces
at
x=0
of
u_{n}(x,t)\doteq\mathcal{S}^{[A_{n}B_{n}]+}_{t}u_{n,0}(x)
,
defined as in (
2.7
).
Similarly, let
u_{l},u_{r}
denote the left and right traces
at
x=0
of
u(x,t)\doteq\mathcal{S}^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}(x)
.
Then, we have:

(2.16)

f_{l}(u_{n,l})\ \ \rightharpoonup\ \ f_{l}(u_{l}),\qquad f_{r}(u_{n,r})\ \ \rightharpoonup\ \ f_{r}(u_{r})\quad\text{weakly \ in}\quad\mathbf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+})\,.

The proof of the Corollary is given in Appendix
A
.

Remark 2.12
.

We point out that, differently
from (
2.16
),
in general
the
{\bf L^{1}}
-convergence

u_{n,l}\to u_{l}

and
u_{n,r}\to u_{r}

fails due to the possible formation of stationary boundary layers
at the interface
x=0
, as one can see in the following

Example 2.13
.

Consider a non critical connection

(A,B)
and the sequence of initial data

u_{n,0}(x)=\begin{cases}\overline{A},&\text{if $x\leq-n^{-1}$},\\
A,&\text{if $x\in\,]-n^{-1},0[$},\\
\overline{B},&\text{if $x\geq 0$},\end{cases}

with

(2.17)

\overline{A}\doteq({f_{l}}_{\mid[\theta_{l},+\infty[\,})^{-1}\circ f_{l}(A)\,,\qquad\quad\overline{B}\doteq({f_{r}}_{|\,]-\infty,\theta_{r}]})^{-1}\circ f_{r}(B),

where
f_{|\,I}

denotes the restriction of the function
f
to the interval
I
.
One can immediately check that the
AB
-entropy solution
of (
1.1
), (
1.2
), with initial datum
u_{n,0}
is the stationary solution
u_{n}(t,x)=\mathcal{S}_{t}^{[AB]+}u_{n,0}(x)=u_{n,0}(x)
and that
u_{n}
converges in
{\mathbf{L}^{1}}
to

u(x,t)=\begin{cases}\overline{A}\ \ &\text{if}\quad x<0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\overline{B}\ \ &\text{if}\quad x>0\,.\end{cases}

Moreover, one has
u_{n,l}(t)=A
and
u_{l}(t)=\overline{A}
for every
t>0
.

2.2.
Backward solution operator

In this section we shall first review quickly the concept of
backward solution
operator
for conservation laws with flux
depending only on the state variable, and then
we will introduce the
definition of backward solution
operator associated to a connection
(A,B)
,
for spatially discontinuous flux as in (
1.3
).

2.2.1.
Backward solution operator for conservation laws with space independent flux

The use of the backward-forward method to characterize
the attainable set for
conservation laws was first proposed in
[
32
,
36
]
(see also
[
26
]
in the framework of Hamilton-Jacobi equations).
Because of the regularizing effect of the nonlinear dynamics
of a conservation law

(2.18)

u_{t}+f(u)_{x}=0,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}\,,

with
uniformly convex flux
f(u)
,
the only restriction to
controllability of (
2.18
) at a fixed time
T>0
, when one regards as controls the initial data

(2.19)

u(x,0)=u_{0}(x),\qquad\quad\,x\in\mathbb{R}\,,

is the decay of positive waves.
Therefore it is by now well known
the characterization of the
attainable set

(2.20)

\mathcal{A}(T)=\big\{\omega\;:\;\omega=u(\cdot,T),\;\text{$u$ entropy weak solution of \eqref{eq:convexconslaw}-\eqref{eq:indatum} with $u_{0}\in{\bf L^{\infty}}$}\big\},

in terms of the
Oleǐnik-type inequality

(2.21)

D^{+}\omega(x)\leq\frac{1}{Tf^{\prime\prime}(\omega(x))},\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}\,,

where
D^{+}\omega
denotes the upper Dini derivative of
\omega

(see (
4.9
)).
Similar results in the case of boundary controllability were obtained in
[
3
,
8
,
9
,
33
]
).

A different perspective to address this controllability problem
was introduced in
[
32
,
36
]
,
and consists in constructing
initial data leading to
attainable targets
\omega

at a time horizon
T>0
,
through the definition
of an
appropriate concept of backward solution to (
2.18
).
Namely, letting
\mathcal{S}_{t}^{+}u_{0}(x)
denote the (forward) entropy weak solution of the Cauchy problem (
2.18
)-(
2.19
)
evaluated at
(x,t)
,
it was defined in
[
36
]
an appropriate
backward operator

\mathcal{S}_{T}^{-}:{\bf L^{\infty}}\to{\bf L^{\infty}}
, and proved that a profile
\omega
belongs to
\mathcal{A}(T)

if and only if
\omega=\mathcal{S}_{T}^{+}\circ\mathcal{S}_{T}^{-}\omega
, i.e. if and only if it is a fixed point of the backward-forward operator
\mathcal{S}_{T}^{+}\circ\mathcal{S}_{T}^{-}
(see
[
32
, Corollary 1]
).
Moreover, for
\omega\in\mathcal{A}(T)
, the solution defined as

(2.22)

u^{*}(x,t)\doteq\mathcal{S}_{t}^{+}(\mathcal{S}_{T}^{-}\omega)(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},\ t\in[0,T]\,,

is the unique solution to (
2.18
) that
is locally Lipschitz on the strip
\mathbb{R}\times]0,T[
, and yields
\omega
at time
T
. Equivalently,

(2.23)

u^{*}_{0}\doteq\mathcal{S}_{T}^{-}\omega

is the unique initial datum that produces a solution to (
2.18
)
locally Lipschitz
on
]0,T[
,
yielding
\omega
at time
T
.
The operator
\mathcal{S}_{t}^{-}
, for
t\geq 0
, is defined as follows

(2.24)

\mathcal{S}_{t}^{-}\omega(x)\doteq\mathcal{S}_{t}^{+}\big(\omega(-\ \cdot\,)\big)(-x)\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},\ t\geq 0\,.

In words, we use
\omega(-\ \cdot\,)
as initial datum for the forward operator
\mathcal{S}_{t}^{+}
, we compute the (forward) solution
to (
2.18
), and then we reverse the space variable.

Remark 2.14

(Classical solutions)
.

Throughout the paper by a
classical solution
to a conservation law with space independent flux
u_{t}+f(u)_{x}=0
we mean a locally Lipschitz function
u:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}\times\,]0,+\infty[
, such that

u_{t}(t,x)+f(u(x,t))_{x}=0\qquad\text{for a.e. $(t,x)\in\Omega$}.

Any classical solution is an entropy admissible weak solution.
The function (
2.22
) is a classical solution to (
2.18
).
Sometimes in the literature classical solutions are denoted as
strong solutions
.

Remark 2.15
.

One can easily verify that
the function
\text{w}(x,t)\doteq\mathcal{S}_{t}^{-}\omega(x)
is the entropy weak solution of the Cauchy problem

(2.25)

\begin{cases}\text{w}_{t}-f(\text{w})_{x}=0,\quad&x\in\mathbb{R},\quad t\geq 0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\text{w}(x,0)=\omega(x),\quad&x\in\mathbb{R}.\end{cases}

In fact, by definition (
2.24
) it follows that
\text{w}(x,t)

is a distributional solution
of (
2.25
), since it is obtained from the distributional solution
\mathcal{S}_{t}^{+}\big(\omega(-\ \cdot\,)\big)(x)
of (
2.18
) by the change of variable
x\mapsto-x
.
On the other hand,
since every shock discontinuity
of
\mathcal{S}_{t}^{+}\big(\omega(-\ \cdot\,)\big)(x)
,
connecting a left state
u^{-}
with a right state
u^{+}
, must satisfy the Lax condition
u^{-}>u^{+}

(equivalent to the entropy admissibility criterion since the flux
f(u)
in (
2.18
) is convex, e.g. see
[
25
,
34
]
),
it follows
that the left and right states

u^{-},u^{+}
of
every shock discontinuity in
\text{w}(x,t)

must satisfy the reverse condition

u^{-}<u^{+}
, which is the Lax admissibility condition for (
2.25
), since the flux
-f(\text{w})
is concave.
Finally, we can observe that

\text{w}(x,0)=\mathcal{S}_{0}^{+}\big(\omega(-\ \cdot\,)\big)(-x)=\omega(x)
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
which completes the proof of our claim.

This procedure to characterize the attainable profiles is motivated by the following observation.
Given a target profile

\omega
, if we know that
for any
t\in\,]0,T[\,
,
the map
x\mapsto v(x,t)\doteq\mathcal{S}_{t}^{+}\big(\omega(-\ \cdot\,)\big)(x)

is locally Lipschitz on
\mathbb{R}
,
it would follow that

u(x,t)\doteq v(-x,T-t)=\mathcal{S}_{T-t}^{-}\,\omega(x)

is a classical solution
of (
2.18
)
which attains the target profile

\omega
at time
t=T
,
and starts with the initial datum
u_{0}^{*}
in (
2.23
).
Since classical solutions
of (
2.18
)
are
entropy admissible,
by uniqueness of entropy weak solutions of the Cauchy problem for (
2.18
)
it would follow that
u(x,t)=\mathcal{S}_{t}^{+}u_{0}^{*}(x)=u^{*}(x,t)
.
However, if
v
admits shock
discontinuities,
the function
v(-x,T-t)

fails to be an entropy admissible solution of (
2.18
),
despite still being a weak distributional solution of (
2.18
).
The
one-sided Lipschitz condition (
2.21
)
is precisely equivalent to the property that the map
x\mapsto v(x,t)\doteq\mathcal{S}_{t}^{+}\big(\omega(-\ \cdot\,)\big)(x)

is locally Lipschitz on
\mathbb{R}
,
for all
t\in\,]0,T[\,

(e.g. see
[
7
,
8
]
),
and thus one obtains
the characterization of
the elements of
\mathcal{A}(T)

as fixed points of
the backward-forward operator.

2.2.2.
Backward solution operator in the spatially-discontinuous flux setting

Given a flux
f
as in (
1.3
)
satisfying the assumption (
1.4
),
and a connection

(A,B)
,
let

\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]+}
be the
forward semigroup operator
associated to the connection
(A,B)
, as in Theorem
2.8
.
Observe that,
letting
\overline{A},\overline{B}

be as in
(
2.17
),
the pair
(\overline{B},\overline{A}\,)

turns out to be a connection for the symmetric flux

(2.26)

\overline{f}(x,u)=\begin{cases}f_{r}(u),&x\leq 0,\\
f_{l}(u),&x\geq 0\,,\end{cases}

(see Figure
3
).

u
f_{l}
f_{r}
A
B
\overline{A}
\overline{B}

Figure 3
.
The connection
(\overline{B},\overline{A})
of the symmetric flux
\overline{f}(x,u)
defined in (
2.26
).

Then, letting

\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{t}^{\,[\overline{B}\,\overline{A}\,]+}u_{0}(x)
denote
the unique
\overline{B}\,\overline{A}
-entropy solution
of

(2.27)

\begin{cases}u_{t}+\overline{f}(x,u)_{x}=0&x\in\mathbb{R},\quad t\geq 0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr u(x,0)=u_{0}(x)&x\in\mathbb{R},\end{cases}

evaluated at
(x,t)
,
we shall define the backward
solution operator associated to the connection
(A,B)
in terms of the operator
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{t}^{\,[\overline{B}\,\overline{A}\,]+}
as follows.

Definition 2.16

(
AB
-Backward solution operator).

Given a connection
(A,B)
, the
backward solution operator

associated to
(A,B)
is the map

\mathcal{S}_{(\cdot)}^{\,[AB]-}:[0,+\infty)\times{\bf L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}\to{\bf L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}
, defined by

(2.28)

\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]-}\omega(x)\doteq\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{t}^{\,[\overline{B}\,\overline{A}\,]+}\big(\omega(-\ \cdot\,)\big)(-x)\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},\ t\geq 0\,.

Remark 2.17
.

One can show that the function

\text{w}(x,t)\doteq\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]-}\omega(x)
is the
\overline{A}\,\overline{B}
-entropy solution of the Cauchy problem

(2.29)

\begin{cases}\text{w}_{t}-f(x,\text{w})_{x}=0,\quad&x\in\mathbb{R},\quad t\geq 0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\text{w}(x,0)=\omega(x),\quad&x\in\mathbb{R}.\end{cases}

Notice that in (
2.29
) the flux is
-f(x,\text{w})
,
which is a discontinuous function that
coincides with the
uniformly strictly concave maps
-f_{l}(\text{w}),-f_{r}(\text{w})
, on the left and on the right, respectively, of
x=0
.
As observed in
[
6
, §7]
, in the case
of a two-concave flux as
-f(x,\text{w})
, one replaces
the
\leq
sign with the
\geq
sign, and viceversa,
in the Definition
2.1
of
interface connection.
Thus,
(\overline{A},\,\overline{B})

is indeed a connection for the flux

-f(x,\text{w})
.
The

\overline{A}\,\overline{B}

interface entropy admissibility condition for
\text{w}(x,t)
is formulated
as in (
2.11
).
In order to verify the claim that

\text{w}(x,t)
is the
\overline{A}\,\overline{B}
-entropy solution of the Cauchy problem (
2.29
)
we proceed as in Remark
2.15
.
We first observe that

\text{w}(x,t)
is a distributional solution of (
2.29
),
and that it is entropy admissible
in the regions
\{x<0\}
,
\{x>0\}
.
In fact, by definition (
2.28
),
\text{w}(x,t)

is obtained from
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{t}^{\,[\overline{B}\,\overline{A}\,]+}\big(\omega(-\ \cdot\,)\big)(x)
with the
change of variable
x\mapsto-x
, and we have
\text{w}(x,0)=\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\,[\overline{B}\,\overline{A}\,]+}_{0}\big(\omega(-\ \cdot\,)\big)(-x)=\omega(x)
,
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
Next, we check that
\text{w}(x,t)
satisfies the
\overline{A}\,\overline{B}

entropy condition (
2.11
)
for the two-concave flux
-f(x,\text{w})
, i.e. that,
letting
\text{w}_{l}(t),\text{w}_{r}(t)
denote the left and right traces of
\text{w}(x,t)
at
x=0
, it holds true

(2.30)

\mathrm{sgn}(\text{w}_{r}(t)-\overline{B}\,)\left(-f_{r}(\text{w}_{r}(t))+f_{r}(\,\overline{B}\,)\right)-\mathrm{sgn}(\text{w}_{l}(t)-\overline{A}\,)\left(-f_{l}(\text{w}_{l}(t))+f_{l}(\,\overline{A}\,)\right)\leq 0\quad\text{for a.e. $t>0$}\,.

Observe that
the left and right traces

u_{l}(t),u_{r}(t)

of
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{t}^{\,[\overline{B}\,\overline{A}\,]+}\big(\omega(-\ \cdot\,)\big)(x)
at
x=0
,
satisfy
the
\overline{B}\,\overline{A}
entropy condition (
2.11
) for the flux
\overline{f}
in (
2.26
), that
reads

(2.31)

\mathrm{sgn}(u_{r}(t)-\overline{A}\,)\left(f_{l}(u_{r}(t))-f_{l}(\,\overline{A}\,)\right)-\mathrm{sgn}(u_{l}(t)-\overline{B}\,)\left(f_{r}(u_{l}(t))-f_{r}(\,\overline{B}\,)\right)\leq 0\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$}\,.

On the other hand,
since one obtains
\text{w}(x,t)

from
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{t}^{\,[\overline{B}\,\overline{A}\,]+}\big(\omega(-\ \cdot\,)\big)(x)

reversing the space variables,
we have
u_{l}(t)=\text{w}_{r}(t)
,

u_{r}(t)=\text{w}_{l}(t)

for all
t>0
.
Hence, we recover (
2.30
)
from (
2.31
),
thus completing the proof of the claim.

Remark 2.18
.

We observe that
if
\omega
is an attainable state in
\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)
, it will follow from our results that
the solution
v(x,t)\doteq\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{t}^{\,[\overline{B}\,\overline{A}\,]+}\big(\omega(-\ \cdot\,)\big)(x)

related to the backward solution operator
may well contain a shock discontinuity exiting from the interface
x=0
at a time
\tau<T
.
As a consequence here,
differently from the space-independent flux setting,
the map

x\mapsto v(x,t)

is in general not
locally Lipschitz outside the interface
\{x=0\}
.
In turn, this implies that,
for
\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)
,
the (forward)
AB
-entropy solution defined by

(2.32)

u^{*}(x,t)\doteq\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}\big(\mathcal{S}_{T}^{\,[AB]-}\omega\big)(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},\ t\in[0,T]\,,

will be in general different
from
v(-x,T-t)
on
\mathbb{R}\times[0,T[\,
.
However, exploiting the duality property enjoyed by the forward and backward solution operators
(see §
3
), one can still prove
that
u^{*}(x,T)=v(-x,0)=\omega(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, which shows that

\omega
is a fixed point
of the backward-forward operator
{\mathcal{S}}_{\it T}^{\,[{\it AB}\,]+}\circ\mathcal{S}_{T}^{\,[AB]-}
as stated in Theorem
1.1
.

3.
Technical tools for characterization
of the near-interface wave structure

In this section we
introduce some technical tools needed to
characterize the pointwise constraints satisfied by
the attainable profiles
of (
1.1
)
in intervals containing the origin.
Throughout the section,

f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
will be a
twice continuously differentiable, uniformly convex map,
and we let
\theta
be its unique critical point,
f^{\prime}(\theta)=0
.
Set

(3.1)

\lambda(u,v)\doteq\frac{f(v)-f(u)}{v-u},\qquad u,v\in\mathbb{R},\ u\neq v\,,

and observe that, by the convexity of
f
one has

(3.2)

f^{\prime}(u)<\lambda(u,v)<f^{\prime}(v)\qquad\forall~u<v\in\mathbb{R}\,.

3.1.
Left backward shock (Figure
4
, left)

For every
B>\theta
,
0<\mathsf{R}<T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)
,
we define here:

-

two constants

\boldsymbol{t}[\mathsf{R},B,f]
,
\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]
;

-

a function

t\mapsto\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](t)
,
t\in[\boldsymbol{t}[\mathsf{R},B,f],\,T]
;

which enjoy the following properties
that will be justified in the sequel (see §
3.4
,
5.5
), but that we highlight here to clarify the purpouse of their introduction.
Let
(A,B)
be a connection for a flux
as in (
1.1
),
and let

\overline{A},\overline{B}

be as in
(
2.17
).
Then, the map
t\mapsto\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}](t)

identifies the location of a shock curve
in a
\overline{B}\overline{A}
-entropy solution
of
u_{t}+\overline{f}(x,u)_{x}=0
, with
\overline{f}
as in (
2.26
),
defined on some domain
\Omega\subset\,]-\infty,0]\times[0,+\infty[\,
.
Since a
\overline{B}\overline{A}
-entropy solution of (
2.27
) is associated to the backward solution operator (
2.28
),
we will say that
\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]

identifies the location of
a
left backward shock.

This curve starts from the interface
\{x=0\}

at time
t=\boldsymbol{t}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]
,
and reaches
the point
x=\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}](T)

at time
t=T
.
Such a shock discontinuity
has, at time
t=T
,
left state
\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]

and right state
\overline{B}
.
The point
(-\mathsf{R},0)

is the center of a rarefaction fan
located on the left of the curve
x\mapsto(\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}](t),t)
.

We proceed to introduce these definitions as follows. Set

(3.3)

\boldsymbol{t}[\mathsf{R},B,f]\doteq\frac{\mathsf{R}}{f^{\prime}(B)},\qquad\quad\overline{B}\doteq({f}_{|\,]-\infty,\theta]})^{-1}\circ f(B)\,.

Then, consider
the Cauchy problem

(3.4)

\begin{cases}{y}^{\prime}(t)=\lambda\Big(\!(f^{\prime})^{-1}\big(\frac{y(t)+\mathsf{R}}{t}\big),\overline{B}\,\Big),\qquad t\geq\boldsymbol{t}[\mathsf{R},B,f]\,,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr y(\boldsymbol{t}[\mathsf{R},B,f])=0.\end{cases}

By (
3.1
), the differential equation in (
3.4
)
ensures that, for all
t\geq\boldsymbol{t}[\mathsf{R},B,f]
, the pair
\big((f^{\prime})^{-1}(\frac{y(t)+\mathsf{R}}{t}),\,\overline{B}\,\big)

satisfies the Rankine-Hugoniot condition with
slope
y^{\prime}(t)
for the conservation law
u_{t}+f(u)_{x}=0
.
Observe that, since

g(t,y)\doteq\lambda\big((f^{\prime})^{-1}\big(\frac{y+\mathsf{R}}{t}\big),\,\overline{B}\,\big)
is locally
Lipschitz continuous in
y
,
by classical arguments
it admits a unique solution
\boldsymbol{y}(t)

defined on some maximal interval

[t[\mathsf{R},B,f],\,\tau[
.
On the other hand,
because of (
3.2
) we have

(3.5)

g(t,y)>f^{\prime}(\overline{B})\qquad\forall~t\in\big[\boldsymbol{t}[\mathsf{R},B,f],\,\min\{\tau,T\}\big[\,,\ y>-\mathsf{R}+T\cdot f^{\prime}(\overline{B})\,,

and hence,
since
f^{\prime}(\overline{B})<0
,
it follows that

(3.6)

\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{y}(t)&>\big(\min\{\tau,T\}-t[\mathsf{R},B,f]\big)\cdot f^{\prime}(\overline{B})\\
&\geq\min\{\tau,T\}\cdot f^{\prime}(\overline{B})\end{aligned}\qquad\quad\forall~t\in\big[t[\mathsf{R},B,f],\,\min\{\tau,T\}\big[\,.

In turn, (
3.6
) implies that

\tau>T
.
Then,
we will
denote by

\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f]:\big[\boldsymbol{t}[\mathsf{R},B,f],\,T\big]\ \to\ \,]-\infty,0[\,,\qquad t\mapsto\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](t)\,,

the unique solution to (
3.4
)
defined on the interval
\big[\boldsymbol{t}[\mathsf{R},B,f],\,T\big]
.
Notice that
t\mapsto\frac{d}{dt}{\boldsymbol{y}}[\mathsf{R},B,f](t)
is strictly decreasing, and
\frac{d}{dt}{\boldsymbol{y}}[\mathsf{R},B,f](t)\leq 0

for all
t\in[\boldsymbol{t}[\mathsf{R},B,f],\,T]
.
Hence, by (
3.6
)
with
\min\{\tau,T\}=T
,
the terminal point satisfies
\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)\in\,]T\cdot f^{\prime}(\overline{B}),0[\,.

Next, we set

(3.7)

\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]\doteq(f^{\prime})^{-1}\left(\frac{\mathsf{R}+\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)}{T}\right)\,.

Observe that, by construction,
\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)
and
\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]
depend continuously on the
parameters
\mathsf{R}
and
B
,
and that we have

(3.8)

{\overline{B}<~}\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]<B\,.

3.2.
Right backward shock (Figure
5
, right)

Symmetrically to §
3.1
,
for every
A<\theta
,
T\cdot f^{\prime}(A)<\mathsf{L}<0
,
we define here:

-

two constants

\boldsymbol{s}[\mathsf{L},A,f]
,
\boldsymbol{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f]
;

-

a function

t\mapsto\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f](t)
,
t\in[\boldsymbol{s}[\mathsf{L},A,f],\,T]
;

which enjoy the following properties
that we highlight here as in §
3.1
to clarify the purpouse of their introduction
(but we will justify them in the sequel, see §
3.5
,
5.5
).
The map
t\mapsto\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f_{l}](t)

identifies the location of a shock curve
in a
\overline{B}\overline{A}
-entropy solution
of
u_{t}+\overline{f}(x,u)_{x}=0
, with
\overline{f}
as in (
2.26
),
defined on some domain
\Omega\subset[0,+\infty[\,\times[0,+\infty[\,
.
Since a
\overline{B}\overline{A}
-entropy solution of (
2.27
) is associated to the backward solution operator (
2.28
),
we will say that
\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f_{l}]

identifies the location of
a
right backward shock.

This curves starts from the interface
\{x=0\}

at time
t=\boldsymbol{s}[\mathsf{L},A,f_{l}]
,
and reaches
the point
x=\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f_{l}](T)

at time
t=T
.
Such a shock discontinuity
has, at time
t=T
, left state
\overline{A}
and
right state

\boldsymbol{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f_{l}]
.
The point
(-\mathsf{L},0)

is the center of a rarefaction fan
located on the right of the curve
t\mapsto(\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f_{l}](t),t)
.

We proceed to introduce these definitions as follows. Set

(3.9)

\boldsymbol{s}[\mathsf{L},A,f]\doteq\frac{\mathsf{L}}{f^{\prime}(A)},\qquad\quad\overline{A}\doteq({f}_{|\,[\theta,+\infty[})^{-1}\circ f(A)\,.

Then, let

\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f]:[\boldsymbol{s}[\mathsf{L},A,f],T]\ \to\ \,]0,+\infty[

denote the unique solution to the Cauchy problem

(3.10)

\begin{cases}{x}^{\prime}(t)=\lambda\left(\!(f^{\prime})^{-1}\big(\frac{x(t)+\mathsf{L}}{t}\big),\overline{A}\,\right),\qquad t\in\big[\boldsymbol{s}[\mathsf{L},A,f],\,T\big],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr x(\boldsymbol{s}[\mathsf{L},A,f])=0.\end{cases}

By (
3.1
), the differential equation in (
3.10
)
ensures that, for all
t\geq\boldsymbol{s}[\mathsf{L},A,f]
, the pair
\big(\overline{A},\,(f^{\prime})^{-1}(\frac{x(t)+\mathsf{L}}{t})\,\big)

satisfies the Rankine-Hugoniot condition with
slope
x^{\prime}(t)
for the conservation law
u_{t}+f(u)_{x}=0
.
The terminal point
\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f](T)

depends continuously on the parameters
\mathsf{L}
,
A
, and
satisfies
\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f](T)\in\big]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(\overline{A})\big[
.
Moreover,
the map
t\mapsto\frac{d}{dt}{\boldsymbol{x}}[\mathsf{L},A,f](t)
is strictly increasing. Next, we define the quantity

(3.11)

\boldsymbol{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f]\doteq(f^{\prime})^{-1}\left(\frac{\mathsf{L}+\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f](T)}{T}\right)\,,

which depends continuously on
\mathsf{L}

and
A
, and satisfies

(3.12)

A<\boldsymbol{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f]{~<\overline{A}}\,.

\mathbf{t}[\mathsf{R},\ B,\ f]
\overline{B}
\mathbf{y}[\mathsf{R},\ B,\ f]
t\ =\ T
B
-\mathsf{R}
t
x
\overline{B}
x
t
B
-\mathbf{y}[\mathsf{R},\ B,\ f]
\mathbf{\tau}[\mathsf{R},\ B,\ f]
t\ =\ T
\mathsf{R}

Figure 4
.
The dual solutions
\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](\cdot)
(left) and
\boldsymbol{x}[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T),\overline{B},f](\cdot)
(right) of the Cauchy problems (
3.4
), (
3.10
). This represents the statement of Lemma
3.1

\mathbf{\sigma}[L,\ A,\ f]
A
\mathsf{L}
t\ =\ T
\overline{A}
-\mathsf{L}
t
x
A
x
t
\overline{A}
-\mathbf{x}[\mathsf{L},\ A,\ f](T)
\mathbf{s}[\mathsf{L},\ A,\ f]
t\ =\ T
\mathbf{x}[\mathsf{L},\ A,\ f](T)

Figure 5
.
The dual solutions
\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f](\cdot)
(right) and
\boldsymbol{y}[\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f](T),\overline{A},f](\cdot)
(left) of the Cauchy problems (
3.4
), (
3.10
). This represents the statement of
Lemma
3.1

3.3.
Duality of forward and
backward shocks

The definitions of backward shocks given in
§
3.1
-
3.2
turn out to be dual one of the other, as clarified by the following:

Lemma 3.1
.

With the notations introduced in §
3.1
-
3.2
,
for every
B>\theta
, the following holds.
The maps

(3.13)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}[\cdot\,,B,f](T):\ ]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)[\,\ \to\ ]T\cdot f^{\prime}(\overline{B}),0[\,,\qquad\mathsf{R}\mapsto\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}[\cdot\,,\overline{B},f](T):\ ]T\cdot f^{\prime}(\overline{B}),0[\,\ \to\ ]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)[\,,\qquad\mathsf{L}\mapsto\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},\overline{B},f](T)

are
increasing,
and one is the inverse of the other, i.e.
it holds true

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{x}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T),\overline{B},f\big](T),\qquad\forall~\mathsf{R}\in\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)[,

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{y}\big[\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},\overline{B},f](T),B,f\big](T),\qquad\forall~\mathsf{L}\in\,]T\cdot f^{\prime}(\overline{B}),0[\,.

Moreover, one has

(3.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathsf{R}\to 0+}\,\boldsymbol{y}[R,B,f](T)

\displaystyle=T\cdot f^{\prime}(\overline{B}),\qquad\lim_{\mathsf{R}\to T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)-}\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathsf{L}\to 0-}\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},\overline{B},f](T)

\displaystyle=T\cdot f^{\prime}(B),\qquad\lim_{\mathsf{L}\to T\cdot f^{\prime}(\overline{B})+}\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},\overline{B},f](T)=0.

Proof.

1.
We will prove only the first equality in (
3.14
),
the proof of the second one being entirely similar.
Fix
\mathsf{R}\in\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)[
, and consider the polygonal
region (the blue set in Figure
4
) defined by

(3.16)

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle\doteq\Delta_{1}\cup\Delta_{2},

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}

\displaystyle\doteq\Big\{(x,t)\in\,]-\infty,0[\,\times\,]0,T[\,\;:\;\mathsf{L}\!-\!(T-t)\cdot f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u})<x<\mathsf{L}\!-\!(T-t)\cdot f^{\prime}(\bar{B}),\ \boldsymbol{t}<t<T\Big\},

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}

\displaystyle\doteq\Big\{(x,t)\in\,]-\infty,0[\,\times\,]0,T[\,\;:\;\mathsf{L}\!-\!(T-t)\cdot f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u})<x<(t-\boldsymbol{t})\cdot\!f^{\prime}(\,B\,),\ 0\leq t\leq\boldsymbol{t}\Big\},

where
\boldsymbol{u}\doteq\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]

is the constant in (
3.7
),
\boldsymbol{t}\doteq\boldsymbol{t}[\mathsf{R},B,f]

is defined as in (
3.3
) and
\mathsf{L}\doteq\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)
.
Observe that the function
v:\Delta\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

(3.17)

v(x,t)\doteq\begin{cases}\overline{B}&\text{if}\qquad\gamma(t)<x<0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr(f^{\prime})^{-1}\bigg(\dfrac{x+\mathsf{R}}{t}\bigg)\quad&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

is locally Lipschitz continuous
and satisfies the equation (
2.18
)
at every point
(x,t)\in\Delta
outside the curve
\gamma(\cdot)\doteq\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](\cdot)
.
Moreover, because of the construction of
\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](\cdot)
,

u
satisfies the Rankine-Hugoniot conditions along
the curve
\gamma
.
Therefore
v(x,t)
is a distributional solution
of (
2.18
) on
\Delta
.
Hence, applying the divergence theorem
to the piecewise smooth vector field

(v,f(v))
on
\Delta
,
and setting
\tau_{1}\doteq T-\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f]/f^{\prime}(\overline{B})
, we find

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\big(f(\overline{B})-\overline{B}f^{\prime}(\overline{B})\big)(T-\tau_{1})+\big(\boldsymbol{u}\,f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u})-f(\boldsymbol{u})\big)T+

\displaystyle\big(f(\,B\,)-Bf^{\prime}(\,B\,)\big)\,\boldsymbol{t}+f(B)(\tau_{1}-\boldsymbol{t})\,.

Then, observing that
f(B)=f(\overline{B})
and that
f^{\prime}(\,B\,)\boldsymbol{t}=\mathsf{R}
, we find

(3.18)

\overline{B}\,\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)+B\,\mathsf{R}-\big(\boldsymbol{u}\,f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u})-f(\boldsymbol{u})\big)T-f(B)T=0\,.

Since
f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u})=(\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)+\mathsf{R})/T
,
and because the Legendre transform
f^{*}
of
f

satisfies the identity

f^{*}(f^{\prime}(u))=u\,f^{\prime}(u)-f(u)\qquad\forall~u\,,

(e.g. see [§A.2]
[
21
]
), we derive
from (
3.18
) the identity

(3.19)

\qquad\overline{B}\,\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)+B\,\mathsf{R}-f^{*}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)+\mathsf{R}}{T}\right)\,T-f(B)\,T=0\qquad\forall~\mathsf{R}\in\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B))[\,.

2.

Next, consider the polygonal
region (the red set in Figure
5
with
A=\overline{B}
and
\overline{A}=B
) defined by

(3.20)

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle\doteq\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}

\displaystyle\doteq\Big\{(x,t)\in\,]0,+\infty[\,\times\,]0,T[\,\;:\;\boldsymbol{x}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T),\overline{B},f\big](T)\!-\!(T-t)\cdot f^{\prime}(B)<x<

\displaystyle<\boldsymbol{x}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T),\overline{B},f\big](T)\!-\!(T-t)\cdot f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{v}),\ \boldsymbol{s}<t<T\Big\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}

\displaystyle\doteq\Big\{(x,t)\in\,]0,+\infty[\,\times\,]0,T[\,\;:\;(t-\boldsymbol{s})\cdot\!f^{\prime}(\,\overline{B}\,)<x<

\displaystyle<\boldsymbol{x}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T),\overline{B},f\big](T)\!-\!(T-t)\cdot f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{v}),\ 0\leq t\leq\boldsymbol{s}\Big\},

where
\boldsymbol{v}\doteq\boldsymbol{v}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T),\overline{B},f\big]

is the constant defined as in (
3.11
), with

\mathsf{L}=\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)
,
A=\overline{B}
and
\boldsymbol{s}=\boldsymbol{s}[\mathsf{L},A,f]
.
Observe that the function
u:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

(3.21)

u(x,t)\doteq\begin{cases}B&\text{if}\qquad 0<x<\gamma(t),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr(f^{\prime})^{-1}\bigg(\dfrac{x+\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)}{t}\bigg)\quad&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

with
\gamma(t)\doteq\boldsymbol{x}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T),\overline{B},f\big](t)
,
is a distributional solution
of (
2.18
) on
\Gamma

for the symmetric arguments of the previous point.
Then, repeating the same type of
analysis of above for the piecewise smooth vector field

(u,f(u))
on
\Gamma
, one finds
the identity

(3.22)

\begin{aligned} &B\,\boldsymbol{x}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T),\overline{B},f\big](T)+\overline{B}\,\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)+\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr&\quad-f^{*}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{x}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T),\overline{B},f\big](T)+\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)}{T}\right)T-f(B)\,T=0\end{aligned}\qquad\forall~\mathsf{R}\in\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B))[\,.

Notice that, by
definition of the function

\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](\cdot)

in §
3.1
,
the terminal value satisfies
\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)\in\,]T\cdot f^{\prime}(\overline{B}),0[
,
for all
\mathsf{R}\in\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B))[
. In turn, from the
definition of

\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f]

in §
3.2
,
with
A=\overline{B}
,
and
\mathsf{L}=\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)
,
it follows that

(3.23)

\boldsymbol{x}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T),\overline{B},f\big](T)\in\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)[\,,\ \qquad\forall~\mathsf{R}\in\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B))[\,.

3.

We fix now
\mathsf{R}\in\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B))[\,
, and we
consider the map

\Upsilon:\ ]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)[\ \to\mathbb{R}
, defined by

(3.24)

\Upsilon(x)\doteq\overline{B}\,\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)+B\,x-f^{*}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)+x}{T}\right)T-f(B)\,T\,.

Observe that, by (
3.19
), (
3.22
), (
3.23
), one has

(3.25)

\Upsilon(\mathsf{R})=\Upsilon\Big(\boldsymbol{x}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T),\overline{B},f\big](T)\Big)=0\,.

Hence, it is sufficient to show that
\Upsilon
admits only one zero in the interval
]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)[
to conclude the proof of the first equality in (
3.14
).
To this end, differentiating
\Upsilon

and recalling the well known property of the Legendre transform (e.g. see
[
21
, §A.2]
),

(f^{*})^{\prime}(p)=(f^{\prime})^{-1}(p)\qquad\forall~p\,,

we find

(3.26)

\displaystyle\Upsilon^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=B-(f^{\prime})^{-1}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)+x}{T}\right)

\displaystyle=(f^{\prime})^{-1}\left(\frac{0+Tf^{\prime}(B)}{T}\right)-(f^{\prime})^{-1}\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)+x}{T}\right)\,.

Since
\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)<0
,
x<T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)
, and because
f^{\prime}
is strictly increasing
as
f^{\prime}
, we deduce from (
3.26
)
that
\Upsilon^{\prime}(x)>0
for all
x\in\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B))[\,
.
Therefore
\Upsilon
is strictly increasing in the interval
\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B))[\,
, completing the proof of of the first equality in (
3.14
).

4.

We show now that the map
\mathsf{R}\mapsto\boldsymbol{y}(\mathsf{R})\doteq\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)
is strictly increasing in the interval
\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)[
. Differentiating (
3.19
) with respect to
\mathsf{R}
, we obtain

(3.27)

\left[\overline{B}-(f^{\prime})^{-1}\Big(\frac{y(\mathsf{R})+\mathsf{R}}{T}\Big)\right]\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}(\mathsf{R})=(f^{\prime})^{-1}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{y}(\mathsf{R})+\mathsf{R}}{T}\Big)-B\qquad\forall~\mathsf{R}\in\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B))[\,.

Since
T\cdot f^{\prime}(\,\overline{B}\,)<\boldsymbol{y}(\mathsf{R})<0
and
0<\mathsf{R}<T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)
,
because
f^{\prime}
is strictly increasing we deduce

\overline{B}<(f^{\prime})^{-1}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{y}(\mathsf{R})}{T}\Big)<(f^{\prime})^{-1}\Big(\frac{\boldsymbol{y}(\mathsf{R})+\mathsf{R}}{T}\Big)<(f^{\prime})^{-1}\Big(\frac{\mathsf{R}}{T}\Big)<B\,,

which, together with (
3.27
), implies that
\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}(\mathsf{R})>0

for all
R\in\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B))[
, as wanted.
In turn, since
\mathsf{L}\mapsto\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},\overline{B},f](T)
is the inverse of
\mathsf{R}\mapsto\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)
, this implies that
\mathsf{L}\mapsto\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},\overline{B},f](T)
is strictly increasing as well in its domain, and
that the image of the maps

\boldsymbol{y}[\cdot\,,B,f](T)
,
\boldsymbol{x}[\cdot\,,\overline{B},f](T)
, in (
3.13
) are the sets
\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B))[\,
and
\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)[
, respectively.
This, together with the monotonicity of the maps
\boldsymbol{y}[\cdot\,,B,f](T)
,
\boldsymbol{x}[\cdot\,,\overline{B},f](T)
,
in particular implies the one-sided limits in (
3.15
),
thus concluding the proof of the Lemma.
∎

Remark 3.2
.

As a consequence of Lemma
3.1

and of the monotonicity of
f^{\prime}
, we find that
the maps

(3.28)

\mathsf{R}\mapsto\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f],\qquad\quad\mathsf{L}\mapsto\boldsymbol{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f],

defined as in (
3.7
)
and (
3.11
),
are strictly increasing, and that we have

(3.29)

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathsf{R}\to 0+}\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]=\overline{B},\qquad\lim_{\mathsf{R}\to T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)-}\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]=B,

\displaystyle\lim_{\mathsf{L}\to 0-}\boldsymbol{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f]=\overline{A},\qquad\ \lim_{\mathsf{L}\to T\cdot f^{\prime}(A)+}\boldsymbol{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f]=A.

This implies that
the functions

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}[\cdot,\cdot,f]:\ ]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)[\,\times\,]\theta,+\infty[\,\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}[\cdot,\cdot,f]:\ ]T\cdot f^{\prime}(A),0[\,\times\,]\theta,+\infty[\,\to\mathbb{R}

can be extended to continuous function on
[0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)]\times

\,]\theta,+\infty[
and

[T\cdot f^{\prime}(A),0]\times\,]\theta,+\infty[
, setting

(3.30)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}[0,B,f]

\displaystyle=\overline{B},\qquad\quad\boldsymbol{u}[T\cdot f^{\prime}(B),B,f]=B,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}[0,A,f]

\displaystyle=\overline{A},\qquad\quad\ \boldsymbol{v}[T\cdot f^{\prime}(A),A,f]=A.

Moreover, one has

(3.31)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]<B

\displaystyle\forall~\mathsf{R}\in\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)[\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f]>A

\displaystyle\forall~\mathsf{L}\in\,]T\cdot f^{\prime}(A),0[\,.

3.4.
Right forward shock-rarefaction wave pattern (Figure
4
, right)

For every
B>\theta
,
0<\mathsf{R}<T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)
,
we define now:

-

a constant
\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f]
;

-

a function

(x,t)\mapsto u[\mathsf{R},B,f](x,t),

(x,t)\in\Gamma[\mathsf{R},B,f]
;

with the following properties.
When
f=f_{r}
, the function

u[\mathsf{R},B,f](x,t)
defines a (forward) solution
associated to the operator

\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]+}
, which contains
a shock
starting
from the interface
\{x=0\}
at time

t=\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]
.
The location of such a shock is given by the map
t\mapsto\boldsymbol{x}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}](T),\overline{B},f_{r}\big](t)
,
where

\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]
and
\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},\overline{B},f_{r}]

with
\mathsf{L}=\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}](T)
,
are the backward shocks
of a backward solution
associated to the operator

\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]-}

introduced in §
3.1
-
3.2
.
Because of Lemma
3.1
,
the shock

t\mapsto\boldsymbol{x}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}](T),\overline{B},f_{r}\big](t)

reaches the point
x=R

at time
t=T
.
We can regard

\boldsymbol{x}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}](T),\overline{B},f_{r}\big]
as
the “dual shock”
of the backward shock
\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]
.

We proceed to introduce these definitions as follows.
With the same notations of §
3.1
-
3.2
,
for every
B>\theta
,
0<\mathsf{R}<T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)
,
we set

(3.32)

\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f]\doteq\boldsymbol{s}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T),\;\overline{B},\;f\big]=\frac{\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T)}{f^{\prime}(\,\overline{B}\,)}\,.

Notice that, by the construction in §
3.1
,
and because of Lemma
3.1
,

\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f]
depends continuously on the parameters
\mathsf{R},B
,
the image of the map
\mathsf{R}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f]
,
\mathsf{R}\in\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)[\,
, is the set
\,]0,T[\,
, and
\mathsf{R}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f]
is decreasing.

Next, we denote by
\Gamma[\mathsf{R},B,f]\subset(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}
the polygonal set (the pink set in Figure
4
)

(3.33)

\Gamma[\mathsf{R},B,f]\doteq\Gamma_{1}[\mathsf{R},B,f]\cup\Gamma_{2}[\mathsf{R},B,f]\,,

with

(3.34)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}[\mathsf{R},B,f]

\displaystyle\doteq\Big\{(x,t)\in\,]0,+\infty[\,\times\,]0,T[\,\;:\;\mathsf{R}-(T-t)\cdot f^{\prime}(B)<x<\mathsf{R}-(T-t)\cdot f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f]<t<T\Big\},

\displaystyle\Gamma_{2}[\mathsf{R},B,f]

\displaystyle\doteq\Big\{(x,t)\in\,]0,+\infty[\,\times\,]0,T[\,\;:\;-(\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f]\!-t)\cdot\!f^{\prime}(\,\overline{B}\,)<x<\mathsf{R}\!-\!(T-t)\cdot f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]),

\displaystyle 0\leq t\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f]\Big\}.

Then, set
\gamma(t)\doteq\boldsymbol{x}\big[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f](T),\overline{B},f\big](t),

and denote by
\mathsf{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]:\Gamma[\mathsf{R},B,f]\to\mathbb{R}
the function defined by

(3.35)

\mathsf{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f](x,t)\doteq\begin{cases}\!B&\text{if}\qquad 0<x<\gamma(t),\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\!(f^{\prime})^{-1}\bigg(\dfrac{x-\mathsf{R}+T\cdot f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f])}{t}\bigg)&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Notice that, by (
3.10
) and (
3.14
),
one has
\gamma(\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f])=0
,

\gamma(T)=\mathsf{R}
.
Moreover, by the same arguments of the
proof of Lemma
3.1
it follows that

u[\mathsf{R},B,f](x,t)

is a distributional solution of (
2.18
)
on
\Gamma[\mathsf{R},B,f]
. Furthermore,
since

t\mapsto\gamma^{\prime}(t)

is strictly increasing
as observed in §
3.2
, it follows
that also the
map

t\mapsto\dfrac{\gamma(t)-\mathsf{R}+T\cdot f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f])}{t}

is strictly increasing.
Therefore,
by virtue of (
3.14
),
and relying on (
3.31
), we find

(3.36)

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to\gamma(t)+}\mathsf{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f](x,t)

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{x\to\gamma(T)+}\mathsf{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f](x,T)

\displaystyle=\lim_{x\to\mathsf{R}+}\mathsf{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f](x,T)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]<B

\displaystyle=\lim_{x\to\gamma(t)-}\mathsf{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f](x,t)\qquad\forall~t\in[\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f],T]\,,

which shows that the
Lax entropy condition
is satisfied along the curve
(t,\gamma(t))
,
t\in[\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f],T]
.
Since the flux in (
2.18
) is strictly convex,
this proves that

u[\mathsf{R},B,f](x,t)

provides an entropy weak solution of (
2.18
)
on the region
\Gamma[\mathsf{R},B,f]
.
Notice that, by (
3.2
), from (
3.36
) we deduce in particular that
f^{\prime}(B)>\lambda\big(\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f],B\big)=\gamma^{\prime}(T)
,
which in turn, by the strict monotonicity of

\dot{\gamma}(t)
, yields

(3.37)

f^{\prime}(B)>\gamma^{\prime}(t)\qquad\forall~t\in[\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f],T]\,.

Hence, relying on (
3.37
), we find

(3.38)

f^{\prime}(B)>\frac{\gamma(T)-\gamma(\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f])}{T-\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f]}=\frac{\mathsf{R}}{T-\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f]}\,.

3.5.
Left forward rarefaction-shock wave pattern (Figure
5
, left)

Symmetrically to §
3.4
,
for every
A<\theta
,
T\cdot f^{\prime}(A)<\mathsf{L}<0
,
we define here:

-

a constant
\boldsymbol{\sigma}[\mathsf{L},A,f]
;

-

a function

(x,t)\mapsto\mathsf{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f](x,t),

(x,t)\in\Delta[\mathsf{L},A,f]
;

with the following properties.
When
f=f_{l}
, the function

\mathsf{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f](x,t)
defines a (forward) solution
associated to the operator

\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]+}
, which contains
a shock
starting
from the interface
\{x=0\}
at time

t=\boldsymbol{\sigma}[\mathsf{L},A,f]
.
The location of such a shock is given by the map
t\mapsto\boldsymbol{y}\big[\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f_{l}](T),\;\overline{A},\;f_{l}\big](t)
,
where

\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f_{l}]
and
\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},\overline{A},f_{l}]

with
\mathsf{R}=\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f_{l}](T)
,
are the backward shocks
of a backward solution
associated to the operator

\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]-}

introduced in §
3.1
-
3.2
.
Because of Lemma
3.1
,
the shock

t\mapsto\boldsymbol{y}\big[\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f_{l}](T),\;\overline{A},\;f_{l}\big](t)

reaches the point
x=L

at time
t=T
.
We can regard
\boldsymbol{y}\big[\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f_{l}](T),\;\overline{A},\;f_{l}\big]
as
the “dual shock”
of the backward shock
\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f_{l}]
.

We proceed to introduce these definitions as follows.
With the same notations of §
3.1
-
3.2
,
for every
A<\theta
,
T\cdot f^{\prime}(A)<\mathsf{L}<0

we set

(3.39)

\boldsymbol{\sigma}[\mathsf{L},A,f]\doteq\boldsymbol{t}\big[\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f](T),\;\overline{A},\;f\big]=\frac{\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f](T)}{f^{\prime}(\,\overline{A}\,)}\,.

By the construction in §
3.2
,
and because of Lemma
3.1
,

\boldsymbol{\sigma}[\mathsf{L},A,f]
depends continuously on the parameters
\mathsf{L},A
, the image of the map

\mathsf{L}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\sigma}[\mathsf{L},A,f]
,
\mathsf{L}\in\,]T\cdot f^{\prime}(A),0[\,
, is the set
\,]0,T[\,
,
and
\mathsf{L}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\sigma}[\mathsf{L},A,f]

is increasing.

Next, we denote by
\Delta[\mathsf{L},A,f]\subset(0,T)\times\mathbb{R}
the polygonal set (the blue set in Figure
5
)

(3.40)

\Delta[\mathsf{L},A,f]\doteq\Delta_{1}[\mathsf{L},A,f]\cup\Delta_{2}[\mathsf{L},A,f]\,,

with

(3.41)

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}[\mathsf{L},A,f]

\displaystyle\doteq\Big\{(x,t)\in\,]-\infty,0[\,\times\,]0,T[\,\;:\;\mathsf{L}-(T-t)\cdot f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f])<x<\mathsf{L}-(T-t)\cdot f^{\prime}(A),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\sigma}[\mathsf{L},A,f]<t<T\Big\},

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}[\mathsf{L},A,f]

\displaystyle\doteq\Big\{(x,t)\in\,]-\infty,0[\,\times\,]0,T[\,\;:\;\mathsf{L}\!-\!(T-t)\cdot f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f])<x<-(\boldsymbol{\sigma}[\mathsf{L},A,f]\!-t)\cdot\!f^{\prime}(\,\overline{A}\,),

\displaystyle 0\leq t\leq\boldsymbol{\sigma}[\mathsf{L},A,f]\Big\}.

Then, set
\gamma(t)\doteq\boldsymbol{y}\big[\boldsymbol{x}[\mathsf{L},A,f](T),\overline{A},f\big](t)
,
and denote by
\mathsf{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f]:\Delta[\mathsf{L},A,f]\to\mathbb{R}
the function defined by

(3.42)

\mathsf{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f](x,t)\doteq\begin{cases}\!A&\text{if}\qquad\gamma(t)<x<0,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\!(f^{\prime})^{-1}\bigg(\dfrac{x-\mathsf{L}+T\cdot f^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f])}{t}\bigg)&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Observe that, by (
3.4
) and (
3.14
),
one has
\gamma(\boldsymbol{\sigma}[\mathsf{L},A,f])=0
,

\gamma(T)=\mathsf{L}
.
With the same arguments of §
3.4
, it follows that
v[\mathsf{L},A,f](x,t)
provides an entropy weak solution of (
2.18
)
on the region
\Delta[\mathsf{L},A,f]
,
and that we have

(3.43)

f^{\prime}(A)<\frac{\gamma(T)-\gamma(\boldsymbol{\sigma}[\mathsf{L},A,f])}{T-\boldsymbol{\sigma}[\mathsf{L},A,f]}=\frac{\mathsf{L}}{T-\boldsymbol{\sigma}[\mathsf{L},A,f]}\,.

Remark 3.3
.

The constant
\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]
defined in §
3.1

is crucial to characterize the
jump of an attainable profile

\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)

at the point

\mathsf{R}\doteq\inf\big\{R>0\;:\;x-T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime}(\omega(x+))\geq 0\quad\forall\;x\geq R\big\},

when
\mathsf{R}\in\,]0,T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime}(B){[}
.
The state
\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]
is constructed so to be the largest right state that one can achieve
at
(\mathsf{R},T)
with a shock that
isolates
the interface
\{x=0\}
from the semiaxis
\{x>0\}
. In fact,
the constant
\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f]
with
f=f_{r}
, identifies
a unique state
\boldsymbol{u}

<B
that has the
property:

-

If
\omega=\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]+}_{T}u_{0}
, and
u(t,x)=\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}(x)
admits a shock generated in
\{x\geq 0\}

at some time
t=\tau
,
and reaching the point
(\mathsf{R},T)
, then letting

\gamma(t)
,
t\in[\tau,T]
, denote the location of such a shock, one has

(3.44)

u_{\gamma}\doteq\lim_{t\to T-}u(t,\gamma(t)+)\leq(f_{r}^{\prime})^{-1}(\mathsf{R}/T)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad u_{\gamma}\leq\boldsymbol{u}\,.

In particular, one has
u_{\gamma}=\boldsymbol{u}

in (
3.44
) only in the case
where
\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}

coincides in the polygonal region
\Gamma[\mathsf{R},B,f]
with the right forward shock-rarefaction pattern described in section
3.4
.
By definition of
u_{\gamma}
it follows that either

\omega(\mathsf{R}+)=u_{\gamma}
,
or else there is another jump
connecting
u_{\gamma}

with
\omega(\mathsf{R}+)
which
must satisfy the Lax entropy condition
\omega(\mathsf{R}+)<u_{\gamma}
.
Therefore, as a consequence of (
3.44
)
we find a necessary condition
for the attainability of
\omega
at time
T
given by

(3.45)

\omega(\mathsf{R}+)\leq\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]\,,

(see (
4.13
) of Theorem
4.3

and the proof in §
5.2.3
).
The interesting fact is that,
in the case
\mathsf{R}\in\,]0,T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime}(B){[}
,
condition (
3.45
),
together with the condition

(3.46)

\omega(x)\geq B\qquad\forall~x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[\,,

(see (
4.14
), (
4.15
), of Theorem
4.3
),
is also sufficient to
guarantee the existence of
an
AB
-entropy solution
u(x,t)

that satisfies

(3.47)

u(x,T)=\omega(x)\qquad\quad\forall~x\in]0,\mathsf{R}],\qquad u(\mathsf{R},T)=\omega(\mathsf{R}+).

To illustrate this claim,
in view of the definitions introduced in the previous sections we proceed as follows.

-

By solving (
3.4
) one determines the end point
\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}](T)
of a “left backward shock” (Figure
4
, left). The map
t\mapsto\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}](t)
represents the position of a shock in a

\overline{B}\overline{A}
-entropy solution
(which is associated to the backward solution operator

\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]-}
,
see Definition
2.16
);

-

given the final position
\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]

of the “backward shock” , one considers the solution
t\mapsto\gamma(t)\doteq\boldsymbol{x}[\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}],\overline{B},f_{r}](t)
to (
3.10
), when
\mathsf{L}=\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]
,
A=\overline{B}
,
f=f_{r}
(see Figure
4
, right). This map represents the position of a shock in a “forward solution”, i.e. in an
AB
-entropy solution associated to the (forward) operator

\mathcal{S}^{\,[AB]+}
in (
2.14
). Actually, we will show in §
5.5
, using the results of this section, that
(t,\gamma(t))

is the location
of a shock of
\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}u_{0}
, with
u_{0}=\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]-}\omega
.

-

once determined the point
\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}](T)
, one defines
\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]
as the state realizing the slope
(\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{,}B,f_{r}](T)+\mathsf{R})/T
(see (
3.7
)):

f_{r}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}])=\frac{\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}](T)+\mathsf{R}}{T};

-

thanks to Lemma
3.1
, we know that the final position at time
T
of the shock
\gamma(t)

satisfies

\gamma(T)=\mathsf{R}.

Using this procedure, if a profile
\omega
satisfies the conditions (
3.45
)-(
3.46
),
we will show in §
5.4
-
5.5
that we can construct admissible
AB
-shocks
that produce at time
T
the given jump in the profile
\omega
at position
\mathsf{R}
.

Entirely symmetric considerations hold for the state
\boldsymbol{v}[\mathsf{L},A,f]
defined in §
3.2
(see Figure
5
).
As a byproduct of this analysis we will obtain that attainable profiles are fixed points of the backward forward solution operator, as stated in Theorem
1.1
.

4.
Statement of the main results

Conditions (1), (2) of Theorem
1.1
will be shown to be equivalent by proving
that they are both equivalent to a characterization of the
attainable set
\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)
in (
1.5
) via
Oleǐnik-type inequalities and state constraints.
To present these results we need
to introduce some furhter notations.

Given a flux
f(x,u)
as in (
1.3
), we
will use the notations

f_{l,-}^{-1}\doteq({f_{l}}_{\mid(-\infty,\theta_{l}]})^{-1}
,

f_{r,-}^{-1}\doteq({f_{r}}_{\mid(-\infty,\theta_{r}]})^{-1}
, for the inverse of the restriction of

f_{l}
,
f_{r}
to their decreasing part, respectively,
and

f_{l,+}^{-1}\doteq({f_{l}}_{\mid[\theta_{l},+\infty)})^{-1}
,

f_{r,+}^{-1}\doteq({f_{r}}_{\mid[\theta_{r},+\infty)})^{-1}
, for the inverse of the restriction of

f_{l}
,
f_{r}
to their increasing part, respectively.
Then, we set

(4.1)

\pi_{l,\pm}\doteq f_{l,\pm}^{-1}\circ f_{l}\,,\qquad\quad\pi_{r,\pm}\doteq f_{r,\pm}^{-1}\circ f_{r}\,,\qquad\quad\pi_{l,\pm}^{r}\doteq f_{l,\pm}^{-1}\circ f_{r}\,,\qquad\quad\pi_{r,\pm}^{l}\doteq f_{r,\pm}^{-1}\circ f_{l}\,.

Moreover, in connection with a
function
\omega:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

we define the quantities

(4.2)

\displaystyle\mathsf{R}[\omega,f_{r}]

\displaystyle\doteq\inf\big\{R>0\;:\;x-T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime}(\omega(x))\geq 0\quad\forall\;x\geq R\big\},

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}[\omega,f_{l}]

\displaystyle\doteq\sup\big\{L<0\;:\;x-T\cdot f_{l}^{\prime}(\omega(x))\leq 0\quad\forall\;x\leq L\big\},

and, if
\mathsf{L}[\omega,f_{l}]\in\,]T\cdot f_{l}^{\prime}(A),0[
,
we set

(4.3)
