\liminf_{n\to\infty}\omega_{n}(\mathsf{R}_{n}+)\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R}_{n},B_{n},f_{r}]\,.

Then, combining (
5.157
), (
5.158
),
with (
5.153
),
and recalling (
3.8
)
with
f=f_{r}
,
we derive

(5.159)

\omega(\mathsf{R}+)\leq\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]<B\,,

which, together with (
4.54
)
(established in Step 2),
proves (
4.56
) in the case

\widehat{\mathsf{R}}=\mathsf{R}
.

Thus, because of (
5.148
),
it remains to analyze the case

\widehat{\mathsf{R}}>\mathsf{R}
.
Let

\vartheta_{n}^{-}

denote the
minimal
backward characteristic
for
u_{n}
starting from

(\mathsf{R}_{n},T)

and lying in the domain
x>0
.
Recalling the definition
(
4.2
), (
5.145
)
of
\mathsf{R}_{n}

this is a map

\vartheta_{n}^{-}:\ ]\tau_{n},T]\to\ ]0,+\infty[\,,

\tau_{n}\geq 0
,
with the property that
\lim_{t\to\tau_{n}}\vartheta_{n}^{-}(t)=0
.
By possibly taking a subsequence, we may assume that
\{\tau_{n}\}_{n}
converges to some
\overline{\tau}\geq 0
.
Observe that
\vartheta_{n}^{-}

are genuine characteristics which, up to a subsequence, converge to a genuine characteristic
\vartheta^{-}:\ ]\overline{\tau},T]\to\ ]0,+\infty[\,

for
u
, starting from

(\vartheta^{-}(T),T)=(\widehat{\mathsf{R}},T)

(see proof of Lemma
C.1
in Appendix
C
).
The trajectory of
\vartheta^{-}
is a segment
with slope
f^{\prime}_{r}(u(\widehat{\mathsf{R}}-,T))=f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(\widehat{\mathsf{R}}-))
.
Therefore, if
\overline{\tau}>0
,
it follows that

f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(\widehat{\mathsf{R}}-))>\widehat{\mathsf{R}}/T
, which
by definition of
\mathsf{R}
implies
\mathsf{R}\geq\widehat{\mathsf{R}}
,
contradicting the assumption
\widehat{\mathsf{R}}>\mathsf{R}
.
Hence, it must be
\overline{\tau}=0
,

\lim_{t\to 0}\vartheta^{-}(t)=0
,
and the trajectory of
\vartheta^{-}
is a segment joining the point
(\widehat{\mathsf{R}},T)

with the origin
(0,0)
.
Since
\mathsf{R}<\widehat{\mathsf{R}}
and because backward characteristics
cannot intersect in the domain
x>0,t>0
,
this in turn implies that
the slope
f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(\mathsf{R}+))

of the maximal
backward characteristic for
u
starting at

(\mathsf{R},T)
must be
greater or equal than
\mathsf{R}/T
. On the other hand,
by definition (
4.2
), (
5.144
)
of
\mathsf{R}
, we have

f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(\mathsf{R}+))\leq\mathsf{R}/T
, and hence it follows
that

(5.160)

f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(\mathsf{R}+))={\mathsf{R}}/{T}.

Observe now that,
applying
Theorem
4.3
-(ii) or
Theorem
4.11
-(ii)’ for
\omega_{n}
,
in the case of the non critical connections
(A_{n},B_{n})
, we
know that (
5.156
) is verified.
Moreover, because of
\mathsf{R}<\widehat{\mathsf{R}}=\lim_{n}\mathsf{R}_{n}

we may assume that
\mathsf{R}<\mathsf{R}_{n}

for
n
sufficiently large. Hence,
by virtue of (
5.143
),
we can extract a subsequnce of
\{\omega_{n}\}
that converges to
\omega
for almost every
x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[
,
and thus we derive from (
5.133
), (
5.156
) that
\omega(\mathsf{R}+)\geq B
.
This inequality, together with (
5.160
), yields

(5.161)

\mathsf{R}\geq T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime}(B)\,,

proving the implication (
4.56
) also in the case
\widehat{\mathsf{R}}>\mathsf{R}
.

Step 4.

(
\mathsf{L}=0
,
\mathsf{R}>0
, proof of (
4.57
):
\mathsf{R}>T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime}(B)\ \ \Rightarrow\ \omega(\mathsf{R}+)\leq\omega(\mathsf{R}-)
)
.

By virtue of (
5.133
), (
5.148
), we may assume that

(5.162)

\mathsf{R}_{n}>T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime}(B_{n})\qquad\forall~n\,.

Then, applying (
4.17
)
of Theorem
4.3
-(ii) or (
4.57
) of Theorem
4.11
-(ii)’ for
\omega_{n}
and the non critical connections
(A_{n},B_{n})
, we derive

(5.163)

\omega_{n}(\mathsf{R}_{n}-)\geq\omega_{n}(\mathsf{R}_{n}+)\qquad\forall~n\,.

On the other hand, if
\widehat{\mathsf{R}}=\mathsf{R}
,
invoking
(
C.4
),
(
C.5
)
of Lemma
C.1
in Appendix
C
,
we deduce as in Step 3 that

(5.164)

\omega({\mathsf{R}}-)\geq\limsup_{n}\omega_{n}(\mathsf{R}_{n}-)\,,\qquad\quad\omega({\mathsf{R}}+)\leq\liminf_{n}\omega_{n}(\mathsf{R}_{n}+)\,.

Then, (
5.163
)-(
5.164
)
together
yield
\omega(\mathsf{R}-)\geq\omega(\mathsf{R}+)
, proving (
4.57
)
in the case
\widehat{\mathsf{R}}=\mathsf{R}
.
Instead, if
\widehat{\mathsf{R}}>\mathsf{R}
, we can assume that

{\mathsf{R}}_{n}>\mathsf{R}
for all
n
sufficiently large.
Then, observe that
applying (
4.12
), (
4.14
), (
4.15
),
of Theorem
4.3
, or (
4.53
) of Theorem
4.11
, for
\omega_{n}
and the non critical connections
(A_{n},B_{n})
, we deduce

(5.165)

\omega_{n}(\mathsf{R}-)\geq\omega_{n}(\mathsf{R}+)\qquad\forall~n\,.

Hence, with the same arguments of above we find that

(5.166)

\omega({\mathsf{R}}-)\geq\limsup_{n}\omega_{n}(\mathsf{R}-)\,,\qquad\quad\omega({\mathsf{R}}+)\leq\liminf_{n}\omega_{n}(\mathsf{R}+)\,,

which, together with (
5.165
), yields
\omega(\mathsf{R}-)\geq\omega(\mathsf{R}+)
, completing the proof of (
4.57
).

Step 5.

(
\mathsf{L}=0
,
\mathsf{R}>0
, proof of (
4.53
):
D^{+}\omega(x)\leq h[\omega,f_{l},f_{r}](x)
in
\,]0,\mathsf{R}[\,
,
and of the existence of
\omega(0\pm)

)
.

Applying
Theorem
4.3
-(i) or
Theorem
4.11
-(i)’ for
\omega_{n}
in the case of the non critical connections
(A_{n},B_{n})
,
we know that

(5.167)

D^{+}\omega_{n}(x)\leq h[\omega_{n},f_{l},f_{r}](x)\qquad\forall~x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}_{n}[\,.

As shown in
[
6
, Lemma 4.4]
,
the inequality (
5.167
) is equivalent to
the fact that the maps

(5.168)

\phi_{n}(x)\doteq-\tau_{n}(x)\cdot f^{\prime}_{l}\circ\pi_{l,+}^{r}(\omega_{n}(x)),\qquad\quad\tau_{n}(x)\doteq T-\frac{x}{f_{r}^{\prime}(\omega_{n}(x))}\,,\qquad x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}_{n}[\,,

are, respectively, nondecreasing
and decreasing.
Since by (
5.148
) it holds
\lim_{n}\mathsf{R}_{n}\geq\mathsf{R}
, relying on (
5.143
) we deduce that, up to a subsequence,
\{\omega_{n}\}_{n}

converges to
\omega

for almost every
x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[
. In turn, this implies that
the sequences
\{\phi_{n}\}_{n}
,
\{\tau_{n}\}_{n}
, converges
for almost every
x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[
to the maps

(5.169)

\phi(x)\doteq-\tau(x)\cdot f^{\prime}_{l}\circ\pi_{l,+}^{r}(\omega(x)),\qquad\quad\tau(x)\doteq T-\frac{x}{f_{r}^{\prime}(\omega(x))}\,,\qquad x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[\,.

Then, the monotonicity of each
map
\phi_{n}(x)
and
\tau_{n}(x)
,
imply the same monotonicity of the maps
\phi(x),\tau(x)

defined in (
5.169
).
Namely,
\phi
is a nondecreasing map and
\tau

is a decreasing map.
But this is equivalent to the inequality (
4.53
), relying again on
[
6
, Lemma 4.4]
.

Next, we observe that the monotonicity of the maps
x\mapsto\phi(x)
,
x\mapsto\tau(x)
, readily implies the existence of the one-sided limit
\omega(0+)
. In fact, since
\phi

and
\tau
are monotone, it follows that the limits
\phi(0+)
,
\tau(0+)
do exist.
On the other hand, observing that
the map
\omega\mapsto f^{\prime}_{l}\circ\pi_{l,+}^{r}(\omega)
,
\omega\geq B
, is invertible, by (
5.169
) we can write

\omega(x)=\big[f^{\prime}_{l}\circ\pi_{l,+}^{r}\big]^{-1}\bigg(-\frac{\phi(x)}{\tau(x)}\bigg)\qquad\forall~x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[\,.

Therefore, since the limit for
x\to 0+
of the right hand side exists,
it follows that the limit
\omega(0+)
exists as well.
Finally, concerning the existence of
\omega(0-)
,
given any sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n}\subset\,]-\infty,0[\,

of points of continuity for
\omega
such that
x_{n}\to 0
, consider the backward genuine characteristics
\vartheta_{n}
for
u
starting at
(x_{n},T)
. Because of the assumption
\mathsf{L}=0
, and by definition (
4.2
), (
5.144
) of
\mathsf{L}
, it follows that

\vartheta_{n}
never cross the interface

x=0
.
Observe that
\{\vartheta_{n}\}_{n}
is a sequence of
Lipschitz continuous functions with a uniform Lipschitz constant,
defined on
[0,T]
and lying in the semiplane
\{x<0\}
.
Hence, by Ascoli-Arzelà Theorem,
we can assume that, up to a subsequence,
\{\vartheta_{n}\}_{n}
converges uniformly to some Lipschitz continuous function
\vartheta:\ [0,T]\to\ \,]-\infty,0[\,.

Therefore, with the same arguments of
the proof of Lemma
C.1
in Appendix
C
,
since uniform limit of genuine characteristics is a genuine characteristic,
and because genuine characteristics
cannot intersect in
\{x<0\}
,
we deduce that

\vartheta
is the minimal
backward characteristic for u
in
\{x\leq 0\}

starting at
(0,T)
.
Moreover,
\vartheta
has slope
\vartheta^{\prime}=\lim_{n}\vartheta^{\prime}_{n}=\lim_{n}f^{\prime}_{l}(\omega(x_{n}))
.
In turn, this implies that
\lim_{n}\omega(x_{n})=(f^{\prime}_{l})^{-1}(\theta^{\prime})
.
Since this limit is independent on the choice of
x_{n}
we deduce that the one-sided limit
\omega(0-)

exists and
\omega(0-)=(f^{\prime}_{l})^{-1}(\theta^{\prime})
.

Step 6.

(
\mathsf{L}=0
,
\mathsf{R}>0
, proof of (
4.55
):
\omega(0-)\geq\pi^{r}_{l,+}(\omega(0+)
)
.

Let
x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[\,
be a point of continuity for
\omega
,
and consider the backward genuine characteristics for
u

starting at
(x,T)
,
defined by

(5.170)

\vartheta_{x}(t)\doteq x-(T-t)\cdot f^{\prime}_{r}((\omega(x))\qquad t\in\,]\tau(x),T]\,,

with

(5.171)

\tau(x)\doteq T-\frac{x}{f_{r}^{\prime}(\omega(x))}\,,

so that one has
\lim_{t\to\tau(x)}\vartheta_{x}(t)=0
.
Observe that the inequality (
4.53
)
(established at
Step 3
)
implies
that the function
\tau(x)
is decreasing. On the other hand,
because of (
5.146
), the slopes of
\vartheta_{x}
are uniformly bounded by
\sup_{|u|\leq\overline{C}}|f^{\prime}_{r}(u)|
.
Therefore,
letting
\{x_{n}\}_{n}\subset\,]0,\mathsf{R}[\,
be a sequence of points of continuity for
\omega
, such that
x_{n}\to 0
,
it follows that

(5.172)

\lim_{n}\tau(x_{n})=T\,.

Notice that, since

\vartheta_{x_{n}}
are genuine
characteristics, we have

(5.173)

\omega(x_{n})=u_{r}(\tau(x_{n}))\qquad\forall~n\,.

Since, by definition (
4.1
)
one has
\pi^{r}_{l,+}(u)\geq\theta_{l}
for any
u
, we may assume that, up to a subsequence,
either

(5.174)

\pi^{r}_{l,+}(\omega(x_{n}))=\theta_{l}\qquad\forall~n\,,

or

(5.175)

\pi^{r}_{l,+}(\omega(x_{n}))>\theta_{l}\qquad\forall~n\,.

In the first case (
5.174
) we deduce
that

(5.176)

\pi^{r}_{l,+}(\omega(0+))=\lim_{n}\pi^{r}_{l,+}(\omega(x_{n}))=\theta_{l}\,,

which yields (
4.55
)
observing that, by definition (
4.2
), (
5.144
),

\mathsf{L}=0
implies
f^{\prime}_{l}(\omega(0-))\geq 0
,
which is equivalent to

\omega(0-)\geq\theta_{l}
.
In the second case (
5.175
)
observe that, since the map

\tau
in (
5.171
) is decreasing, and because

x_{n}
are points of continuity
for
\omega
, then

\tau(x_{n})
are points of continuity for
u_{r}
.
Hence, by the interface entropy condition (
2.13
) it follows that
\tau(x_{n})
are points of continuity also for
u_{l}
.
Then,
we can trace the backward genuine characteristics for
u
starting
at
(0,\tau(x_{n}))
,
that, because of (
5.173
),
are defined by

(5.177)

\vartheta_{n}(t)\doteq(t-\tau(x_{n}))\cdot f^{\prime}_{l}\circ\pi^{r}_{l,+}(\omega(x_{n})),\qquad t\in[0,\tau(x_{n})]\,.

Notice that
\{\vartheta_{n}\}_{n}
is a sequence of
Lipschitz continuous functions with a uniform Lipschitz constant,
defined on uniformly bounded intervals
[0,\tau(x_{n})]
.
Hence, by Ascoli-Arzelà Theorem,
and because of (
5.172
), we can assume that, up to a subsequence,
\{\vartheta_{n}\}_{n}
converges uniformly to some Lipschitz continuous function
\vartheta:\ [0,T]\to\ [0,+\infty[\,.

Therefore, with the same arguments of
the proof of Lemma
C.1
in Appendix
C
,
since uniform limit of genuine characteristics is a genuine characteristic
we deduce that

\vartheta
is a
backward genuine characteristic starting at
(0,T)
,
that has slope
f^{\prime}_{l}\circ\pi^{r}_{l,+}(\omega(0+))
.

On the other hand
the minimal backward characteristic starting at
(0,T)
has slope
f^{\prime}_{l}(\omega(0-))
.
Since the slope of the minimal backward characteristic is larger than the slope of any other backward characteristic passing through the same point, it follows that
f^{\prime}_{l}(\omega(0-))\geq f^{\prime}_{l}\circ\pi^{r}_{l,+}(\omega(0+))
,
which implies (
4.55
).
This concludes the proof of
this step.

Step 7.

(
\mathsf{L}=0
,
\mathsf{R}>0
, proof of (
4.52
):
D^{+}\omega(x)\leq\frac{1}{T\cdot f^{\prime\prime}_{l}(\omega(x))}

in
\,]\!-\!\infty,0[
, and
D^{+}\omega(x)\leq\frac{1}{T\cdot f^{\prime\prime}_{r}(\omega(x))}

in
\,]\mathsf{R},+\infty[\,
)
.

Observe that, by
definition (
4.2
), (
5.144
)
of
\mathsf{L},\mathsf{R}
,
and since
\mathsf{L}=0
,
backward characteristics starting at points
(x,T)
,
with
x\in]-\infty,0[\,\cup\,]\mathsf{R},+\infty[
do no intersect the interface
x=0
.
Hence, we recover the Oleǐnik estimates (
4.52
)
as a classical property
of solutions
to conservation laws with strictly convex flux, which
follows from the fact that genuine characteristics never intersect at positive times.
This completes the proof of the existence of
\omega(0\pm)
and that
\omega
satisfies conditions (i)’-(ii)’
of Theorem
4.11
.

Step 8.

(
\mathsf{L}<0
,
\mathsf{R}=0
, proof of the existence of
\omega(0\pm)
, and of conditions (i)-(ii)
of Theorem
4.11
)
.

The Oleǐnik-type inequalities (
4.46
), (
4.47
), and the existence of
\omega(0\pm)
can be established with the same arguments of Steps 5, 7.
The proofs of (
4.48
), (
4.49
),
are entirely similar to the proofs of (
4.54
),
(
4.55
), in Steps 2 and 6, respectively. Since
A=\theta_{l}
,
and hence
f^{\prime}_{l}(A)=0
, the implication (
4.50
) is trivially verified. Finally, with the same arguments of the proof of (
4.57
) in Step 4
one can recover the inequality
\omega(\mathsf{L}-)\geq\omega(\mathsf{L}+)
, thus proving
the implication
(
4.51
).
Therefore the proof of the existence of
\omega(0\pm)
and that
\omega
satisfies the conditions (i)-(ii) of
Theorem
4.11
is completed.

Step 9.

(
\mathsf{L}=0
,
\mathsf{R}=0
, proof of (
4.62
):
D^{+}\omega(x)\leq\frac{1}{T\cdot f^{\prime\prime}_{l}(\omega(x))}

in
\,]\!-\!\infty,0[
, and
D^{+}\omega(x)\leq\frac{1}{T\cdot f^{\prime\prime}_{r}(\omega(x))}

in
\,]0,+\infty[
,

of the existence of
\omega(0\pm)
:
\omega(0-)\geq\pi^{r}_{l,+}(\omega(0+)
,
and of (
4.63
):
\omega(0-)\geq\overline{A}
,
\omega(0+)\leq\overline{B}
)
.

Since
\mathsf{L}=0
,
\mathsf{R}=0
,
by
definition (
4.2
) it follows that
backward characteristics starting at
(x,T)
,
x\in\,]-\infty,0[\,\cup\,]0,+\infty[
,
never intersect the interface
x=0
.
Thus, as observed in Step 7, the Oleǐnik estimates in (
4.62
)
are a classical property
of solutions.

Moreover, with the same arguments of Step 5 one deduces the existence of
\omega(0\pm)
.

Furhter
, the inequality

\omega(0-)\geq\pi^{r}_{l,+}(\omega(0+)
can be established with the same proof of (
4.55
)
in Step 6.
Finally, the inequality

\omega(0+)\leq\overline{B}

is obtained with the same arguments of the proof of (
4.56
)
in Step 3, observing that
by Remark
3.2
we have

\boldsymbol{u}[0,B,f_{r}]=\boldsymbol{u}[0+,B,f_{r}]=\overline{B}
.
The other inequality

\omega(0-)\geq\overline{A}

can derived in entirely similar way.
Therefore the proof of the existence of
\omega(0\pm)
and
that
\omega
satisfies the
conditions (i)-(ii)
of Theorem
4.14
is completed.

Step 10.

(
\mathsf{L}<0<\mathsf{R}
, proof of condition (i) of Theorem
4.9

and of the existence of
\omega(0-)
)
.

The Oleǐnik inequalities (
4.34
)
are a classical property
of solutions
to conservation laws with strictly convex flux
as observed in Step 7.
The proof of the Oleǐnik type inequality (
4.35
) can be recovered with the same limiting procedure of Step 5, passing to the limit the monotonicity of the maps

(5.178)

\phi_{n}(x)\doteq-\tau_{n}(x)\cdot f^{\prime}_{r}\circ\pi_{r,-}^{l}(\omega_{n}(x)),\qquad\quad\tau_{n}(x)\doteq T-\frac{x}{f^{\prime}_{l}(\omega_{n}(x))}\,,\qquad x\in\,]\mathsf{L}_{n},0[\,,

ensured by the Oleǐnik type inequalities satisfied by
\omega_{n}\in\mathcal{A}^{[A_{n}B_{n}]}(T)
,
and
relying on (
5.143
), (
5.148
).
This also shows that the one-sided limit
\omega(0-)
exists, using the monotonicity of the limiting maps
\phi(x)=\lim_{n}\phi_{n}(x)
,
\tau(x)=\lim_{n}\tau_{n}(x)
,
x\in\,]\mathsf{L},0[\,
, as in Step 5.

Step 11.

(
\mathsf{L}<0<\mathsf{R}
, proof
of (
4.37
):
\omega(\mathsf{L}-)\geq\omega(\mathsf{L}+)
,
\omega(0-)=\theta_{l}
)
.

The proof of the first constraint
in (
4.37
) can be obtained with the same procedure of Step 4
(with
\mathsf{L}
in place of
\mathsf{R}
).
Concerning the second constraint in (
4.37
),
notice first that we have
\omega(0-)\leq\theta_{l}
, since otherwise we may consider
a sequence
\{x_{n}\}_{n}
of continuity points for
\omega
, such that
x_{n}\uparrow 0
, and for
n
sufficiently large
the backward characteristics for
u
from
(x_{n},T)

would intersect in
\{x<0\}
the maximal backward characteristic for
u
from the point
(\mathsf{L},T)
, which gives a contradiction. Next, assume that the strict inequality
\omega(0-)<\theta_{l}
holds. Then, let
x<0
be a continuity point of
\omega

sufficiently close to
0

so that
\omega(x)<\theta_{l}
,
and consider the time

\tau(x)=T-\frac{x}{f_{l}^{\prime}(\omega(x))}

at which the backward (genuine) characteristic for
u
from
(x,T)
impacts the interface
x=0
.
Since
x
is a continuity point for
\omega
, by the strict monotonicity of the map
\tau
on
\,]\mathsf{L},0[\,
(derived in Step 9
as in Step 5)
it follows
that
u_{l}(\tau(x))=\omega(x)<\theta_{l}
.
Using also the
AB
-entropy conditions (
2.13
) we then deduce
that
u_{r}(\tau(x))=\pi_{r,-}^{l}(\omega(x))<\theta_{r}
. But this implies that the maximal backward characteristic for
u
from
(0,\tau(x))
intersects in
\{x>0\}
the minimal backward characteristic for
u
from
(\mathsf{R},T)
, which again gives a contradiction, and thus completes the proof of (
4.37
).

Step 12.

(
\mathsf{L}<0<\mathsf{R}
, proof
of (
4.38
):
\omega(x)=B
in
]0,\mathsf{R}[\,
,
\mathsf{R}\in]0,T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime}(B)[
,
of
B\neq\theta_{r}
,
and of (
4.39
):

\omega(\mathsf{R}+)\leq\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]\leq\omega(\mathsf{R}-)
)
.

Towards a proof of (
4.38
), first notice that the maximal backward characteristic
for
u
from
(\mathsf{L},T)
must intersect the
interface
x=0
at a time

(5.179)

\tau_{{}_{\mathsf{L}}}\doteq T-\frac{\mathsf{L}}{f^{\prime}_{l}(\mathsf{L}+)}<T-\frac{\mathsf{R}}{f^{\prime}_{r}(\mathsf{R}-)}.

In fact otherwise, we could consider a point

x>\mathsf{L}
of continuity for
\omega

sufficiently close to
\mathsf{L}
, so that
\tau(x)>T-\mathsf{R}/f^{\prime}_{r}(\mathsf{R}-)
.
But then, by the analysis in Step 11,
the maximal backward characteristic
for
u
from
(0,\tau(x))
would intersect in
\{x>0\}
the minimal backward characteristic for
u
from
(\mathsf{R},T)
, thus giving a contradiction.
Next, observe that at any point
x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[\,

of continuity for
\omega
we have

\omega(x)\geq\theta_{r}
, since otherwise
the backward characteristic for
u
from
(x,T)

would intersect in
\{x>0\}
the minimal backward characteristic for
u
from the point
(\mathsf{R},T)
, which gives a contradiction.
By the
AB
-entropy conditions, and because of the strict monotonicity of the map

\tau(x)=T-\frac{x}{f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(x))},\qquad x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[\,,

(that can be established with the same limiting procedure of Steps 5, 10), it then follows that
\omega(x)\geq B
for all
x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[\,
. Assume now that
\omega(x)>B
for some
x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[\,
continuity point for
\omega
.
Observe that,
because of the non crossing property of characteristics in
\{x>0\}
,
and by (
5.179
),
the backward characteristic for
u
from
(x,T)
impacts the interface
x=0

at time

(5.180)

\tau(x)\geq T-\frac{\mathsf{R}}{f^{\prime}_{r}(\mathsf{R}-)}>\tau_{{}_{\mathsf{L}}}.

Then, relying on the strict monotonicity of the map
\tau
,
and using the
AB
-entropy conditions (
2.13
), we deduce that

u_{r}(\tau(x))=\omega(x)>B
,
u_{l}(\tau(x))=\pi^{r}_{l,+}(\omega(x))>\theta_{l}
.
But, because of (
5.180
), this implies that the minimal backward characteristic for
u
from
(0,\tau(x))
intersects in
\{x<0\}
the maximal backward characteristic for
u
from
(\mathsf{L},T)

which again gives a contradiction. Hence we have shown that

\omega(x)=B
in
]0,\mathsf{R}[\,
.
By definition (
4.2
) of
\mathsf{R}
,
and because of (
5.180
),
this implies
that

\mathsf{R}\in\,]0,T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime}(B)[\,,

and thus completes the proof of (
4.38
).
This also shows that
we must have
B\neq\theta_{r}
.
Furthermore, we can derive (
4.39
)
with exactly the same arguments contained in the proof of (
4.56
)
in Step 3.

Step 13.

(
\mathsf{L}<0<\mathsf{R}
, proof of (
4.36
):

(f^{\prime}_{l})^{-1}(\frac{x}{T-\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]})\leq\omega(x)<\theta_{l}
in
\,]\mathsf{L},0[\,

)
.

Let
\mathsf{L}_{n},\mathsf{R}_{n}
be the constants defined at (
5.145
) as in (
4.2
) and, according with (
4.4
), define

(5.181)

{\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}}_{n}\doteq\big(T-\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R}_{n},B_{n},f_{r}]\big)\cdot f^{\prime}_{l}(A_{n}).

Since (
5.132
), (
5.133
) imply
\lim_{n}f^{\prime}_{l}(A_{n})=0
,
and recalling that
\mathsf{R}_{n}
are bounded
(see Step 1),
it follows that
\lim_{n}{\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}}_{n}=0
.
Thus, recalling also that the
limit
\widehat{\mathsf{R}}
of a subsequence of
\{\mathsf{R}_{n}\}_{n}
satisfies (
5.148
),
we may assume that
\mathsf{L}_{n}<{\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}}_{n}<0<\mathsf{R}_{n}
for
n
sufficiently large.
Then, applying (
4.14
) or (
4.16
) of Theorem
4.3
for
\omega_{n}
in the case of the non critical connections
(A_{n},B_{n})
, we deduce
that

(5.182)

\omega_{n}(x)\leq A_{n}\qquad\forall~x\in\,]{\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}}_{n},\mathsf{L}_{n}[\,,\qquad\quad\omega_{n}({\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}}_{n}\pm)=A_{n}\,.

Therefore, by (
5.181
) the backward characteristic for
u_{n}

starting at
({\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}}_{n},T)
reaches
the interface
x=0
at time
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n}\doteq\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R}_{n},B_{n},f_{r}]
. In turn, this implies that,
for every

x\in\,]\mathsf{L}_{n},\widetilde{L}_{n}[

point of continuity for
\omega_{n}
,
the backward characteristic starting at
(x,T)
must cross the interface
x=0
at a time smaller or equal than
\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n}\doteq\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R}_{n},B_{n},f_{r}]
, since otherwise it would intersect the
backward characteristic for
u_{n}

starting at
({\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}}_{n},T)

in the domain
\{x<0\}
, which gives a contradiction. Thus we have

(5.183)

T-\frac{x}{f^{\prime}_{l}(\omega_{n}(x))}\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n}\,,

for every

x\in\,]\mathsf{L}_{n},\widetilde{L}_{n}[

point of continuity for
\omega_{n}
.
On the other hand, recall that by Lemma
3.1

the map
\mathsf{R}\mapsto\boldsymbol{y}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}](T)<0
is strictly increasing, and hence by (
3.32
)
the map
\mathsf{R}\to\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]
is strictly decreasing.
Therefore,
since
\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]

depends continuously on the parameters
\mathsf{R},B

(see §
3.4
), and because of (
5.148
),
we deduce that

(5.184)

\lim_{n}\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n}=\boldsymbol{\tau}[\,\widehat{\mathsf{R}},B,f_{r}]\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]\,.

Hence, taking the limit in (
5.183
) as
n\to\infty
, and relying
again on (
5.143
), (
5.148
),
we derive

(5.185)

T-\frac{x}{f^{\prime}_{l}(\omega(x))}\leq\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]\qquad\text{for \ a.e.}~x\in\,]{\mathsf{L}},0[\,.

In turn, the inequality (
5.185
)
yields the first inequality in (
4.36
).
On the other hand, if
\omega(x)\geq\theta_{l}

for some
x\in\,]\mathsf{L},0[\,
,
with the same arguments of Step 11
one deduces that the backward characteristic
for
u
starting from
(x,T)

must intersect in
\{x<0\}

the maximal backward characteristic for
u
from the point
(\mathsf{L},T)
, which gives a contradiction. This shows that also the
second inequality in (
4.36
)
is satisfied.
Therefore, the proof of the existence of
\omega(0\pm)
and
that
\omega
satisfies the
conditions (i)-(ii)
of Theorem
4.9
is completed.
This concludes the proof
of the implication
(1)\Rightarrow(3)
for critical connections.

5.8.
Part 3.c -
(3)\Rightarrow(1)
for critical connections

In this Subsection we
rely on the fact

that
Theorem
4.17
holds for connections which are non critical, and in particular
we know (by §
5.2
,
5.3
,
5.4
)
that
Theorems
4.3
,
4.11
,
4.14
,
are verified
for non critical connections.
We will prove that
if
\omega\in\mathscr{A}^{\mathsf{L},\mathsf{R}}

satisfies the conditions of

Theorem
4.9
,
4.11
, or of Theorem
4.14
,
then
\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)

also for a critical connection

(A,B)
satisfying (
5.132
).

Step 1.

Given an element
\omega

of the set
\mathscr{A}^{\mathsf{L},\mathsf{R}}

in (
4.8
), assuming that:

-

if
\mathsf{L}<0
,
\mathsf{R}>0
or viceversa,
\omega
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
4.9
;

-

if
\mathsf{L}=0
,
\mathsf{R}>0
or viceversa,
\omega
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
4.11
;

-

if
\mathsf{L}=0
,
\mathsf{R}=0
,
\omega
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
4.14
;

we will construct a sequence
\{\omega_{n}\}_{n}

of suitable
perturbations
of
\omega

with the property that:

(5.186)

\omega_{n}\in\mathcal{A}^{[A_{n}B_{n}]}(T)\quad\forall~n,\qquad\quad\omega_{n}\to\omega\quad\ \text{in \ $\bf L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})$},

for a sequence of non critical connections
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n}

satisfying (
5.133
)
and

(5.187)

A_{n}<A,\qquad\qquad B_{n}>B\qquad\quad\forall~n\,.

The conditions in (
5.186
) in turn will imply that
\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)
.
In fact, by the validity of Theorem
4.17
in the
non critical
case,
and because of (
5.186
), it holds

(5.188)

\omega_{n}=\mathcal{S}^{[A_{n}B_{n}]+}_{T}\circ\mathcal{S}^{[A_{n}B_{n}]-}_{T}\omega_{n}\qquad\forall\;n\,.

On the other hand, relying on the stability property (iv) of Theorem
2.8
,
and thanks to (
5.186
),
one finds as in §
5.6
that

(5.189)

S^{[A_{n}B_{n}]+}_{T}\circ\mathcal{S}^{[A_{n}B_{n}]-}_{T}\omega_{n}\ \ \rightarrow\ \ \mathcal{S}^{[AB]+}_{T}\circ\mathcal{S}^{[AB]-}_{T}\omega\qquad\text{in}\quad\mathbf{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})\,.

Hence, combining together (
5.186
), (
5.188
), (
5.189
),
we derive

\omega=\mathcal{S}^{[AB]+}_{T}\circ\mathcal{S}^{[AB]-}_{T}\omega\,,

which clearly yields
\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)
.
Therefore, to establish the implication
(3)\Rightarrow(1)
of Theorem
4.17
for non critical connections, it remains to produce a family
\{\omega_{n}\}_{n}
that satisfies (
5.186
).
We shall construct such perturbations of
\omega\in\mathscr{A}^{\mathsf{L},\mathsf{R}}
as suitable

“
(A_{n},B_{n})
admissible envelopes
”
of
\omega
.

We will first consider in Steps 2-8 the case

\mathsf{L}=0
,
\mathsf{R}\geq 0
, while the
symmetric
case
\mathsf{L}<0
,
\mathsf{R}=0
is entirely similar.
Next, we will consider separately the case
\mathsf{L}<0,\mathsf{R}>0
, in Step 9.

Step 2.

We shall assume throughout Steps 2-8 that

(5.190)

\mathsf{L}=\mathsf{L}[\omega,f_{l}]=0,\quad\qquad\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{R}[\omega,f_{r}]\geq 0\,,

and that:
\omega
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
4.11
if
\mathsf{R}>0
;

\omega
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
4.14
if
\mathsf{R}=0
.

We will perturb
\omega

to obtain an attainable profile
\omega_{n}
for the

(A_{n},B_{n})
connection by:

-

shifting
\omega

on the right of
x=0
by a size
\delta_{1,n}
, and
on the left of
x=0
by a size
\delta_{2,n}
;

-

choosing
\delta_{1,n}
so to satisfy the admissibility condition (
4.56
) at
x=\mathsf{R}+\delta_{1,n}
(if
\mathsf{R}+\delta_{1,n}>0
);

-

lifting
\omega
to the value
B_{n}
of the connection when it is below, in the interval

]0,\mathsf{R}+\delta_{1,n}[
,
so to satisfy the admissibility condition (
4.54
)
(if
\mathsf{R}+\delta_{1,n}>0
);

-

inserting a profile of a rarefaction in the interval
]-\delta_{2,n},0[
, so to satisfy the Lax-type admissibility condition (
4.55
)
at
x=0
.

Namely,
consider the function

(5.191)

\omega_{n}(x)\doteq\begin{cases}\omega(x-\delta_{1,n}),&\text{if \ \ $x\geq\mathsf{R}+\delta_{1,n}$},\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\max\{\omega(\mathsf{R}+),B_{n}\},&\text{if \ \ $x\in\,]\mathsf{R},\mathsf{R}+\delta_{1,n}[$},\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\max\{\omega(x),B_{n}\},&\text{if \ \ $x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[$},\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr(f_{l}^{\prime})^{-1}\bigg(\dfrac{x+T\cdot f_{l}^{\prime}\big(\max\{\omega(0-),\;\overline{A}_{n}\}\big)}{T}\bigg),\quad&\text{if \ \ $x\in\,]-\delta_{2,n},0[$},\\
\omega(x+\delta_{2,n}),&\text{if \ \ $x\leq-\delta_{2,n}$}.\end{cases}

with

(5.192)

\displaystyle\delta_{1,n}

\displaystyle\doteq\inf\Big\{\delta\geq 0\ \ :\ \ \text{either}\quad\mathsf{R}+\delta\geq T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime}(B_{n}),

\displaystyle\text{or}\quad\mathsf{R}+\delta<T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime}(B_{n})\quad\text{and}\ \ \omega(\mathsf{R}+)\leq\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R}+\delta,B_{n},f_{r}]\Big\}\,,

\displaystyle\delta_{2,n}

\displaystyle\doteq T\cdot f_{l}^{\prime}\big(\max\{\omega(0-),\;\overline{A}_{n}\}\big)-T\cdot f_{l}^{\prime}(\omega(0-)),

where
\overline{A}_{n}
is defined as in (
2.17
).
Recalling the definitions (
4.2
),
and because of (
5.190
),
we deduce
that

(5.193)

\mathsf{L}_{n}\doteq\mathsf{L}[\omega_{n},f_{l}]=0,\quad\qquad\mathsf{R}_{n}\doteq\mathsf{R}[\omega_{n},f_{r}]=\mathsf{R}+\delta_{1,n}\qquad\quad\forall~n\,.

\mathsf{R}
\mathsf{R}+\delta_{1}^{n}
-\delta_{2}^{n}
\omega,\ \omega^{n}

Figure 19
.
The “candidate” characteristics of
\omega
and of its admissible
A^{n}B^{n}
envelopes
\omega^{n}
(dashed).

Notice that the assumption that

\omega
satisfies conditions (ii)’ of
of Theorem
4.11
or
conditions (ii) of Theorem
4.14
, together with (
5.133
),
imply that

(5.194)

\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{1,n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\delta_{2,n}=0\,.

In fact, if
\mathsf{R}\leq T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime}(B)
,
relying on conditions (
4.56
), (
4.60
) of Theorem
4.11
or on condition (
4.64
) of Theorem
4.14

we deduce that
\omega(\mathsf{R}+)\leq\boldsymbol{u}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]
.
Moreover, we know by Remark
3.2

that
\boldsymbol{u}[\cdot,\cdot,f_{r}]
is continuous in the first two entries.
Therefore, because of (
5.133
), we derive from definition (
5.192
)
that, if
\mathsf{R}\leq T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime}(B)
,
then
\lim_{n}\delta_{1,n}=0
.
On the other hand, if
\mathsf{R}>T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime}(B)
, then it follows
from definition (
5.192
)
and (
5.133
),
that
\delta_{1,n}=0
for sufficiently large
n
.
Next, observe that, since
\mathsf{L}=0
,
by definition (
4.2
)
one has
\omega(0-)\geq\theta_{l}
. On the other hand
by assumptions (
5.132
), (
5.133
)
it follows that
\lim_{n}\overline{A}_{n}=\theta_{l}
.
Therefore, by definition (
5.192
) we deduce that

\lim_{n}\delta_{2,n}=0
.

Because of (
5.133
),
and relying on conditions (ii) of Theorem
4.11
or of Theorem
4.14
,
we deduce that the limit
(
5.194
) implies
the
\mathbf{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
convergence
of
\omega_{n}
to
\omega

as
n\to\infty
. Hence, in order to show that
\omega_{n}
satisfy (
5.186
)
it remains to prove that
\omega_{n}\in\mathcal{A}^{[A_{n}B_{n}]}(T)

for all
n
.
Since we are assuming in particular the validity of the implication
(2)\Rightarrow(1)
of Theorem
4.17
for non critical connections,
in order to establish

\omega_{n}\in\mathcal{A}^{[A_{n}B_{n}]}(T)
it will be sufficient to show that:
if
{\mathsf{R}}_{n}=0
then

\omega_{n}
satisfies conditions (i)-(ii) of Theorem
4.14
;
if
{\mathsf{R}}_{n}>0
then
\omega_{n}
satisfies conditions (i)’-(ii)’ of Theorem
4.11
.
This is established in the steps below distinguishing the cases where
{\mathsf{R}}=0
or

{\mathsf{R}}>0
and
{\mathsf{R}_{n}}=0
or
{\mathsf{R}_{n}}>0
.
Notice that, by definition (
5.191
),
we always have

(5.195)

\omega_{n}({\mathsf{R}}_{n}-)\geq\omega_{n}({\mathsf{R}}_{n}+)\,.

Step 3.

(
\mathsf{R}_{n}>0
,
\mathsf{R}=0
,
proof that
\omega_{n}
satisfies condition (ii)’ of Theorem
4.11
)
.

By definition (
5.191
)
one has

(5.196)

\omega_{n}(0-)=\max\{\omega(0-),\overline{A}_{n}\}\,,\qquad\ \omega_{n}(0+)=\max\{\omega(0+),B_{n}\},\qquad\ \omega_{n}(\mathsf{R}_{n}+)=\omega(\mathsf{0}+)\,,

and

(5.197)

\omega_{n}(x)\geq B_{n}\qquad\ \ \forall x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}_{n}[\,,

while definition (
5.192
), together with (
5.193
), (
5.196
),
(
5.197
),
and (
3.8
) with
f=f_{r}
, yield

(5.198)

{\mathsf{R}}_{n}\in\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}_{r}(B_{n})[\,\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\omega_{n}(\mathsf{R}_{n}+)\leq\boldsymbol{u}[{\mathsf{R}}_{n},B_{n},f_{r}]\leq\omega_{n}(\mathsf{R}_{n}-)\,.

Since
\pi^{r}_{l,+}(B_{n})=\overline{A}_{n}
,
from (
5.196
)
we deduce

(5.199)

\pi^{r}_{l,+}(\omega_{n}(0+))=\max\{\pi^{r}_{l,+}(\omega(0+)),\overline{A}_{n}\}.

Hence (
5.196
), (
5.199
),
imply

(5.200)

\omega_{n}(0-)\geq\pi^{r}_{l,+}(\omega_{n}(0+))\,.

Therefore, if
\mathsf{R}_{n}>0
and
\mathsf{R}=0
, then
conditions (
5.195
), (
5.197
), (
5.198
), (
5.200
)
show that
\omega_{n}
satisfies condition (ii)’ of Theorem
4.11
.

Step 4.

(
\mathsf{R}_{n}>0
,
\mathsf{R}>0
,
proof that
\omega_{n}
satisfies condition (ii)’ of Theorem
4.11
)
.

By definition (
5.191
)
one has

(5.201)

\omega_{n}(0-)=\max\{\omega(0-),\overline{A}_{n}\}\,,\qquad\ \omega_{n}(0+)=\max\{\omega(0+),B_{n}\},\qquad\ \omega_{n}(\mathsf{R}_{n}+)=\omega(\mathsf{R}+)\,,

and

(5.202)

\omega_{n}(x)\geq B_{n}\qquad\ \ \forall x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}_{n}[\,,

while definition (
5.192
), together with (
5.193
), (
5.201
), (
5.202
),
and (
3.8
) with
f=f_{r}
,
yield the implication (
5.198
).
Since we are assuming that
\omega
satisfies condition (
4.55
) of Theorem
4.11
,
relying on (
5.201
)
we deduce as in Step 4
that (
5.199
),
(
5.200
)
hold.
Therefore, if
\mathsf{R}_{n}>0
and
\mathsf{R}>0
, then
(
5.195
), (
5.198
), (
5.200
),
(
5.202
)
show that
\omega_{n}
satisfies condition (ii)’ of Theorem
4.11
.

Step 5.

(
\mathsf{R}_{n}=\mathsf{R}=0
, proof that
\omega_{n}
satisfies condition (ii)
of Theorem
4.14
)
.

By definition (
5.191
),
we have

(5.203)

\omega_{n}(0-)=\max\{\omega(0-),\;\overline{A}_{n}\}\geq\overline{A}_{n},\qquad\quad\omega_{n}(0+)=\omega(0+)\,,

while
definition (
5.192
)
yields

\omega(0+)\leq\boldsymbol{u}[0,B_{n},f_{r}]
.
Since by Remark
3.2

we have

\boldsymbol{u}[0,B_{n},f_{r}]=\overline{B}_{n}

(
\overline{B}_{n}
as in (
2.17
)),
it follows that

(5.204)

\omega_{n}(0+)\leq\overline{B}_{n}.

Moreover, by virtue of (
5.203
)
we deduce (
5.200
).
Hence, if
\mathsf{R}_{n}=0
and
\mathsf{R}=0
, then conditions (
5.200
), (
5.203
), (
5.204
) show that

\omega_{n}
satisfies condition (ii)
of Theorem
4.14
.

Step 6.

(
\mathsf{R}_{n}>0
,
\mathsf{R}\geq 0
,
proof that
\omega_{n}
satisfies (
4.52
)
of Theorem
4.11
)
.

Since we are assuming that
\omega
satisfies either
the Oleǐnik estimates (
4.62
)
of Theorem
4.14
(in case
\mathsf{R}=0
), or
the Oleǐnik estimates
(
4.52
)
of Theorem
4.11
(in case
\mathsf{R}>0
),
computing the Dini derivative
of
\omega_{n}
in (
5.191
), we find

(5.205)

\displaystyle D^{+}\omega_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=D^{+}\omega(x-\delta_{1,n})\leq\frac{1}{T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime\prime}(\omega(x-\delta_{1,n}))}=\frac{1}{T\cdot f_{r}^{\prime\prime}(\omega_{n}(x))}\qquad\forall~x>{\mathsf{R}}_{n}\,,

\displaystyle D^{+}\omega_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=D^{+}\omega(x+\delta_{2,n})\leq\frac{1}{T\cdot f_{l}^{\prime\prime}(\omega(x+\delta_{2,n}))}=\frac{1}{T\cdot f_{l}^{\prime\prime}(\omega_{n}(x))}\qquad\forall~x<-\delta_{2,n}\,,

\displaystyle D^{+}\omega_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T\cdot f_{l}^{\prime\prime}(\omega_{n}(x))}\qquad\quad\forall~x\in[-\delta_{2,n},0[\,.

Observing that
\omega_{n}
is continuous at
x=-\delta_{2,n}
,
we deduce from (
5.205
)
that
\omega_{n}

satisfies the Oleǐnik estimates (
4.52
)
of Theorem
4.11
.

Step 7.

(
\mathsf{R}_{n}>0
,
\mathsf{R}\geq 0
, proof that
\omega_{n}
satisfies (
4.53
)
of Theorem
4.11
)
.

Observe that by definition (
5.191
)
\omega_{n}
is constant in
\,]\mathsf{R},\mathsf{R}_{n}[
.
Therefore, since it holds (
5.195
),
in order to show that
\omega_{n}
satisfies the estimate (
4.53
) on
]0,\mathsf{R}_{n}[
it will be sufficient to show that (
4.53
)
is verified on
]0,\mathsf{R}[
,
assuming that
\mathsf{R}>0
.

As observed in Step 5 of §
5.7
,
the assumption
that
\omega
satisfies condition (
4.53
)
of Theorem
4.11
is equivalent to the
fact that the maps

(5.206)

\phi(x)\doteq-\tau(x)\cdot f^{\prime}_{l}\circ\pi_{l,+}^{r}(\omega(x)),\qquad\quad\tau(x)\doteq T-\frac{x}{f_{r}^{\prime}(\omega(x))}\,,\qquad x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[\,.

are, respectively, nondecreasing
and decreasing.
Then consider the corresponding maps for
\omega_{n}

(5.207)

\phi_{n}(x)\doteq-\tau_{n}(x)\cdot f^{\prime}_{l}\circ\pi_{l,+}^{r}(\omega_{n}(x)),\qquad\quad\tau_{n}(x)\doteq T-\frac{x}{f_{r}^{\prime}(\omega_{n}(x))}\,,\qquad x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[\,,

and compare their values
in two points
0<x_{1}<x_{2}<\mathsf{R}
, of continuity for
\omega
and
\omega_{n}
:

- if
\omega_{n}(x_{i})=\omega(x_{i})
for
i=1,2
, then one clearly has that

\phi_{n}(x_{1})=\phi(x_{1})\leq\phi(x_{2})=\phi_{n}(x_{2})
,

\tau_{n}(x_{1})=\tau(x_{1})>\tau(x_{2})=\tau_{n}(x_{2})
;

- if
\omega_{n}(x_{i})\neq\omega(x_{i})
for
i=1,2
, then by definition (
5.191
) we have
\omega_{n}(x_{i})=B_{n}
for
i=1,2
and therefore
one has
\phi_{n}(x_{1})<\phi_{n}(x_{2})
,
\tau_{n}(x_{1})>\tau_{n}(x_{2})
;

- if
\omega_{n}(x_{1})=\omega(x_{1})

and
\omega_{n}(x_{2})\neq\omega(x_{2})
, then by definition (
5.191
) we have

\omega_{n}(x_{1})\geq B_{n}
,

\omega_{n}(x_{2})=B_{n}
, which implies

f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega_{n}(x_{2}))\leq f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega_{n}(x_{1}))
.
Moreover, since
\omega(x)\geq\theta_{r}
,
\omega_{n}(x)\geq\theta_{r}
,
it follows that

f^{\prime}_{l}\circ\pi_{l,+}^{r}(\omega_{n}(x_{1}))\geq f^{\prime}_{l}\circ\pi_{l,+}^{r}(\omega_{n}(x_{2}))
.
Hence, we derive that

\phi_{n}(x_{1})\leq\phi_{n}(x_{2})

\tau_{n}(x_{1})>\tau_{n}(x_{2})
;

- if
\omega_{n}(x_{1})\neq\omega(x_{1})

and
\omega_{n}(x_{2})=\omega(x_{2})
, then by definition (
5.191
) we have

\omega_{n}(x_{1})=B_{n}>\omega(x_{1})
, which implies

f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega_{n}(x_{1}))>f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(x_{1}))
.
Notice that by definition (
4.2
)
of
\mathsf{R}
it follows that

\omega(x_{1})\geq\theta_{r}
.
Since also
\omega_{n}(x_{1})\geq\theta_{r}
,
we deduce that

f^{\prime}_{l}\circ\pi_{l,+}^{r}(\omega_{n}(x_{1}))>f^{\prime}_{l}\circ\pi_{l,+}^{r}(\omega(x_{1}))
.
Thus, it follows that

\phi_{n}(x_{1})<\phi(x_{1})\leq\phi(x_{2})=\phi_{n}(x_{2})
,

\tau_{n}(x_{1})>\tau(x_{1})>\tau(x_{2})=\tau_{n}(x_{2})
.

Hence, extending the above
estimates to the right
limits of
\omega_{n}
in its points of discontinuity,
we have shown that
it holds true

(5.208)

\phi_{n}(x_{1})\leq\phi_{n}(x_{2}),\qquad\quad\tau_{n}(x_{1})>\tau_{n}(x_{2})\qquad\ \forall~0<x_{1}<x_{2}<\mathsf{R}\,.

In turn, the monotonicity (
5.208
) of

\phi_{n}
,
\tau_{n}

is equivalent to the fact that
\omega_{n}
satisfies (
4.53
), by the same arguments of Step 5 of §
5.7
.

Step 8.

(
\mathsf{R}_{n}=\mathsf{R}=0
,
proof that
\omega_{n}
satisfies (
4.62
)
of Theorem
4.14
)
.

The proof is entirely similar to the one of Step 6,
under the assumption that

\omega
satisfies
the Oleǐnik estimates (
4.62
)
of Theorem
4.14
.

Step 9.

Finally, let us assume

(5.209)

\mathsf{L}=\mathsf{L}[\omega,f_{l}]<0,\quad\qquad\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{R}[\omega,f_{r}]>0\,,

and (because of (
5.132
))
that
\omega
satisfies the conditions (i), (ii) of Theorem
4.9
.
Hence, by virtue of (
5.133
) we may assume also that, for
n
sufficiently large there hold

(5.210)

\mathsf{L}<T\cdot f^{\prime}_{l}(A_{n}),\qquad\qquad\mathsf{R}<T\cdot f^{\prime}_{r}(B_{n})\,.

In a similar way to what is done in Step 2, we will perturb
\omega

to obtain an attainable profile
\omega_{n}
for the

(A_{n},B_{n})
connection by:

-

shifting
\omega

on the right of
x=0
by a size
\delta_{1,n}

-

choosing
\delta_{1,n}
so to satisfy the admissibility condition (
4.13
) at
x=\mathsf{R}+\delta_{1,n}
;

-

dropping
\omega
to the value
A_{n}
of the connection when it is above, in the interval

]\mathsf{L},0[
, so to satisfy the admissibility condition (
4.16
).

Namely,
consider the function

(5.211)

\omega_{n}(x)\doteq\begin{cases}\omega(x-\delta_{1,n}),&\text{if \ \ $x\geq\mathsf{R}+\delta_{1,n}$},\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr B_{n}&\text{if \ \ $x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}+\delta_{1,n}[$},\\
\min\{A_{n},\omega(x)\}&\text{if \ \ $x\in]\mathsf{L},0$[},\\
\omega(x)&\text{if \ \ $x\leq\mathsf{L}$},\end{cases}

with

(5.212)

\delta_{1,n}\doteq\inf\Big\{\delta\in\mathbb{R}\ \ :\ \ \boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R}+\delta,B_{n},f_{r}]=\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]\Big\}\,.

Notice that the definition (
5.212
) is meaningful since
the map
\mathsf{R}\mapsto\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B_{n},f_{r}]
is strictly monotone and continuous, and because the image
of the maps

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tau}[\,\cdot\,,B,f_{r}]

\displaystyle:\,\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B)[\,\ \to\ \,]0,+\infty[\,,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\tau}[\,\cdot\,,B_{n},f_{r}]

\displaystyle:\,\,]0,T\cdot f^{\prime}(B_{n})[\,\ \to\ \,]0,+\infty[\,,

is the set
]0,T[

(see §
3.4
).
Then, recalling the definitions (
4.2
),
(
4.4
), and because of
(
5.210
),
we deduce
that

(5.213)

\mathsf{L}_{n}\doteq\mathsf{L}[\omega_{n},f_{l}]=\mathsf{L},\quad\qquad\mathsf{R}_{n}\doteq\mathsf{R}[\omega_{n},f_{r}]=\mathsf{R}+\delta_{1,n},

and

(5.214)

\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{n}\doteq\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}[\omega_{n},f_{l}]=(T-\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n})\cdot f_{l}^{\prime}(A_{n})=(T-\boldsymbol{\tau})\cdot f_{l}^{\prime}(A_{n}),

where

(5.215)

\boldsymbol{\tau}_{n}\doteq\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R}+\delta_{1,n},B_{n},f_{r}],\qquad\quad\boldsymbol{\tau}\doteq\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}].

Relying on (
5.133
) and
since
\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]

depends continuously on the parameters
\mathsf{R},B

(see §
3.4
),
one deduces that
\lim_{n}\delta_{1,n}=0
, that
\lim_{n}\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{n}=0
,
and that

\omega_{n}
converges to
\omega

in
\mathbf{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
as
n\to\infty
. Hence, as in Step 2 above we conclude that, in order to show the validity of (
5.186
)
it remains to prove that

\omega_{n}
satisfies conditions (i)-(ii) of Theorem
4.3
.

Assuming that
\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{n}\in\,]\mathsf{L},0[
for
n
sufficiently large,
in order to show that
\omega_{n}
satisfies (
4.16
) of Theorem
4.3
it will be sufficient to prove that

(5.216)

A_{n}\leq\omega(x)\qquad\forall~x\in\,]\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{n},0[\,,\qquad\quad A_{n}\leq\omega(\,\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{n}-)\,.

To this end observe that, by definition (
5.214
),
we have
(f^{\prime}_{l})^{-1}(\frac{x}{T-\boldsymbol{\tau}[\mathsf{R},B,f_{r}]})>A_{n}
for all
x\in\,]\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{n},0[\,
.
Thus, the first inequality in (
4.36
) satisfied by
\omega

implies that

\omega(x)>A_{n}
for all
x\in\,]\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{n},0[\,
,
which proves
the first condition in (
5.216
).
Next observe that, since

\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{n}\in\,]\mathsf{L},0[
,
from the first inequality in (
4.36
)
and by definition (
5.214
) it follows

f^{\prime}_{l}(\omega(\,\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{n}-))\geq\frac{\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{n}}{T-\boldsymbol{\tau}}\geq f^{\prime}_{l}(A_{n}),

which
implies
\omega(\,\widetilde{\mathsf{L}}_{n}-)\geq A_{n}
. This completes the proof of (
5.216
) and thus that
\omega_{n}
satisfies (
4.16
).
The verification that
\omega_{n}
satisfies the remaining conditions
in (i)-(ii) of Theorem
4.3

is entirely similar to the one performed in Steps 4, 6, 7 above, and is accordingly omitted.
This concludes the proof
of the implication
(3)\Rightarrow(1)
for critical connections.

Remark 5.2
.

Whenever
\mathrm{Tot.Var.}(\omega)<+\infty
,
the perturbed profiles
\omega_{n}

approximating
\omega

constructed in Step 2 and in Step 9 of §
5.8

may possibly have larger total variation than the one of
\omega
.
However,
\omega_{n}

have always local bounded variation,
even in the case where
\mathrm{Tot.Var.}(\omega)=+\infty
.
In fact, assuming (
5.190
)
and that
\omega
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
4.14
, suppose that
\omega
has unbounded total variation on a right neighborhood of
x=0
.
Then, letting
\{(A_{n},B_{n})\}_{n}

be a sequence of non critical connections satisfying (
5.133
), (
5.187
),
there should exist a sequence of positive values
\rho_{n}\downarrow 0
,
so that

(5.217)

\omega(x)\leq B_{n},\qquad\forall~x\in\,]0,\rho_{n}]\,,

for all
n
sufficiently large. If this is not the case, then there should exist
\overline{\rho}>0
and
\overline{n}
so that
\omega(x)\geq B_{\overline{n}}>\theta_{r}
for all
x\in\,]0,\overline{\rho}]
.
But this in turn would yield
uniform upper bounds on
D^{+}\omega

(and hence on the total variation of
\omega
as well)
on bounded subsets
K
of
[0,+\infty[
,
with the same type of analysis of §
5.3
.
Therefore, because of (
5.217
), by definition (
5.191
) we have

(5.218)

\omega_{n}(x)=B_{n},\qquad\forall~x\in\,]0,\rho_{n}]\,,

for all
n
sufficiently large.
The property (
5.218
)
has precisely the effect to cut the
possible large oscillation of
\omega

occurring in a right neighborhood of
x=0
,
and hence to ensure that
\mathrm{Tot.Var.}(\omega_{n},\,K)<+\infty

for all
n
large.
Clearly, we will have that
\lim_{n}\mathrm{Tot.Var.}(\omega_{n},\,K)=+\infty
.
With entirely similar
arguments one can show that,
if
\omega
satisfies the conditions (i), (ii) of Theorem
4.9
, then the profile
\omega_{n}
defined by (
5.211
) has always local bounded variation.

6.
BV
bounds for
AB
-entropy solutions

We collect in this section the
BV
bounds for solutions, and for the flux of the solutions, that arise as a corollary of our analysis.

Proposition 6.1
.

In the same setting of Theorem
2.8
,
for every
u_{0}\in{\bf L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
and for any bounded set
K\subset\mathbb{R}
,
the following properties are verified.

(i)

For any non critical connection
(A,B)
, there exists a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(A,B,\|u_{0}\|_{{\bf L^{\infty}}},K)>0
such that
it holds true

(6.1)

\mathrm{Tot.Var.}\big(\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}u_{0},\,K\big)\leq\frac{C_{1}}{t}\qquad\forall~t>0\,.

In particular,
any attainable profile

\omega\doteq{\mathcal{S}}_{\it T}^{\,[{\it AB}\,]+}u_{0}
,
u_{0}\in{\bf L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
T>0
,
enjoy the
property (
H
) stated in §
5.1
-
Part 1
.

(ii)

There exists a constant
C_{2}=C_{2}(\|u_{0}\|_{{\bf L^{\infty}}},K)>0
such that,
for any connection
(A,B)
,
it holds true

(6.2)

\begin{aligned} \mathrm{Tot.Var.}\big(f_{l}\circ\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}u_{0},\,K\cap\,]-\infty,0]\big)&\leq\frac{C_{2}}{t},\\
\mathrm{Tot.Var.}\big(f_{r}\circ\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}u_{0},\,K\cap[0,+\infty[\big)&\leq\frac{C_{2}}{t},\end{aligned}\qquad\forall~t>0\,,

where the inequalities are understood to be verified whenever
K\,\cap\,]-\infty,0]\neq\emptyset
, or
K\,\cap[0,+\infty[\,\neq\emptyset
, respectively.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{S}^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}\in\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(t)
and
thanks to the implication
(1)\Rightarrow(3)
of Theorem
4.17
, we know that

\mathcal{S}^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}

satisfies the conditions stated in
Theorem
4.3
,
4.9
,
4.11
, or
4.14
, that cover all possible cases.
We divide the proof in four steps.

Step 1.

(proof of (i)).

In the case of a non critical connection
(A,B)
,
it is well known that for initial data
u_{0}\in BV(\mathbb{R})
, one has
\mathcal{S}^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}\in BV(\mathbb{R})
for all
t>0

(see
[
29
, Lemma 8]

and

[
1
, Theorem 2.13-(iii)]
).

On the other hand
, for initial data
u_{0}\in{\bf L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
we know that

\mathcal{S}^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}

satisfies the Oleǐnik-type inequalities stated in
Theorem
4.3
,
4.11
, or
4.14
.
Thus, since
(A,B)
is a non critical connection,
by the analysis in §
5.3

we deduce that
D^{+}(\mathcal{S}^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0})

satisfies
one-sided uniform upper bounds
as the ones provided by (
5.85
).
In turn, such bounds
yield the existence of uniform bounds on the total increasing variation (and hence on the total variation as well) of
S^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}
on bounded subsets
K
of
[0,+\infty[
,
which depend on
the connection
(A,B)
, on the set
K
, and on
\|u_{0}\|_{{\bf L^{\infty}}}
.
By similar arguments we derive bounds on the
total variation of
S^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}
on bounded subsets of
]-\infty,0]
, which yields (
6.1
),
completing the proof of (i).

Step 2.

(proof of (ii) when
S^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}
satisfies the conditions of
Theorem
4.14
).

Since
S^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}
satisfies the Oleǐnik-type inequalities (
4.62
) of
Theorem
4.14
,
we immediately deduce
a uniform bound
on the total increasing variation
of
S^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}

on bounded sets,
which does not depend on the
values
f^{\prime}_{l}(A),f^{\prime}_{r}(B)
.
In turn, such bounds
yield the existence of uniform bounds on the total increasing variation (and hence on the total variation as well) of
S^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}
on bounded subsets
K
of
[0,+\infty[
,
which depend on
the set
K
and on
\|u_{0}\|_{{\bf L^{\infty}}}
.
By similar arguments we derive bounds on the
total variation of
S^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}
on bounded subsets of
]-\infty,0]
, which yields (
6.1
),
with a constant
C_{1}
that
depends only on
the set
K
and on
\|u_{0}\|_{{\bf L^{\infty}}}
.
In turn, (
6.1
) yields (
6.2
)
relying on the Lipschitzianity of
f_{l},f_{r}
on the set
[-M,M]
,
with
M\doteq\|u_{0}\|_{{\bf L^{\infty}}}
.
This completes the proof of (ii) in
the case where
S^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}
satisfies
the conditions
stated in Theorem
4.14
.

Step 3.

(proof of (ii) when
S^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}
satisfies the conditions of
Theorem
4.11
).

To fix the ideas, we assume that

\omega\doteq S^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}
satisfies
the inequalities (i)’
and the pointwise constraints-(ii)’
stated in Theorem
4.11
.
Notice that, by the same arguments of above, (
4.52
) yields
the estimate (
6.1
)
(and hence also (
6.2
))
for bounded set
K\subset\,]-\infty,0]
or
K\subset[\mathsf{R},+\infty[
.
Then consider a set
K\subset[0,\mathsf{R}]
, with
\mathsf{R}=\mathsf{R}[\omega,f_{r}]

defined as in (
4.2
),
and assume that the inequalities (
4.53
), (
4.54
), are satisfied.
Observe that,
by the uniform convexity (
1.4
) of
f_{l},f_{r}
,
we have

(6.3)

\frac{f^{\prime\prime}_{l}\circ\pi_{l,+}^{r}(u)}{\big[f^{\prime}_{l}\circ\pi_{l,+}^{r}(u)\big]^{2}}\geq c_{1},\qquad\ f^{\prime\prime}_{r}(u)\geq c_{1},\qquad\forall~|u|\leq\|\omega\|_{{\bf L}^{\infty}}\,,

for some constant
c_{1}>0

depending on
\|\omega\|_{{\bf L}^{\infty}}
.
Moreover,
by definition (
4.2
)
of
\mathsf{R}

it holds true

(6.4)

t\cdot f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(x))>x\qquad\forall~x\in[0,\mathsf{R}[\,,\quad t>0\,.

Hence, recalling the definition (
4.6
) of the function
h
,
and relying on (
4.53
),
(
4.54
),
(
6.3
), (
6.4
),
we derive

(6.5)

\displaystyle D^{+}(f_{r}\circ\omega)(x)

\displaystyle=f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(x))\,D^{+}\omega(x)

\displaystyle\leq f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(x))\,h[\omega,f_{l},f_{r}](x)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{[f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(x))]^{2}}{c_{1}\,[f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(x))]^{2}\big(t\cdot f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(x))-x\big)+c_{1}\,x}\qquad\forall~x\in[0,\mathsf{R}[\,.\quad t>0\,.

Towards an estimate of (
6.5
), consider the map

(6.6)

\Phi(x,t,u)\doteq\begin{cases}\dfrac{[f^{\prime}_{r}(u)]^{2}}{[f^{\prime}_{r}(u)]^{2}\big(t\cdot f^{\prime}_{r}(u)-x\big)+x},\quad&\text{if}\quad u>\theta_{r},\\
\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\cr\ 0,\quad&\text{if}\quad u=\theta_{r},\end{cases}\qquad\quad x\in[0,\mathsf{R}[\,,\quad t>0\,.

By direct computations one finds

\Phi_{u}(x,t,u)=\frac{f^{\prime}_{r}(u)\,f^{\prime\prime}_{r}(u)\big(2x-t\,[f^{\prime}_{r}(u)]^{3}\big)}{\Big([f^{\prime}_{r}(u)]^{2}\big(t\cdot f^{\prime}_{r}(u)-x\big)+x\Big)^{2}\ }.

Hence, since
f^{\prime}_{r}(u)\geq 0
for all
u\geq\theta_{r}
, and because
f^{\prime\prime}_{r}(u)>0
for
all
u
, we deduce that,
setting

(6.7)

u_{x,t}\doteq(f^{\prime}_{r})^{-1}\bigg(\sqrt[3]{\frac{2x}{t}\,}\,\bigg),

for all
x,t>0
it holds true

(6.8)

u_{x,t}>\theta_{r},\qquad\quad\Phi_{u}(x,t,u)\begin{cases}\,\geq 0\ \ &\text{if}\quad u\in[\theta_{r},u_{x,t}],\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr\,\leq 0\ \ &\text{if}\quad u\geq u_{x,t}\,.\end{cases}

In turn, (
6.7
), (
6.8
) imply
that
u_{x,t}
is a point of global maximum for the map
u\mapsto\Phi(x,t,u)
,
u\geq\theta_{r}
. On the other hand,
because of
(
6.8
)
we have

(6.9)

t\cdot f^{\prime}_{r}(u_{x,t})>x\quad\Longrightarrow\quad x<\sqrt{2}\,t\,.

Thus we find

(6.10)

\Phi(x,t,u)\leq\Phi(x,t,u_{x,t})=\frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{x}\Big(3\big(\frac{t}{2}\big)^{\frac{2}{3}}-x^{\frac{2}{3}}\Big)}<\frac{1}{\sqrt[3]{x}}\bigg(\frac{2}{t}\bigg)^{\!\!\frac{2}{3}}\,,\qquad\forall~x<\sqrt{2}\,t,\ \ u\geq\theta_{r}\,,

and

(6.11)

\Phi(x,t,u)\leq\frac{[f^{\prime}_{r}(u)]^{2}}{x}\leq\frac{c_{2}}{t}\,,\qquad\forall~x\geq\sqrt{2}\,t,\ \ \theta_{r}\leq u\leq\|\omega\|_{{\bf L}^{\infty}}\,,

for some constant
c_{2}
depending on
\|\omega\|_{{\bf L}^{\infty}}
.
Then, relying on (
6.4
), (
6.5
), (
6.6
), (
6.9
), (
6.10
), (
6.11
),
we derive

(6.12)

D^{+}(f_{r}\circ\omega)(x)\leq\begin{cases}\dfrac{c_{3}}{\sqrt[3]{x}\ t^{\frac{2}{3}}}\ \ &\text{if}\quad x<\sqrt{2}\,t,\quad x\in[0,\mathsf{R}[\,,\\
\vskip 12.0pt plus 4.0pt minus 4.0pt\cr\dfrac{c_{3}}{t}\ \ &\text{if}\quad x\geq\sqrt{2}\,t,\quad x\in[0,\mathsf{R}[\,,\end{cases}

for some other constant
c_{3}
depending on
\|\omega\|_{{\bf L}^{\infty}}
.
Hence, recalling Remark
2.10
, we deduce
that, given
a bounded set
K\subset[0,\mathsf{R}]
,
we have

\int_{K}D^{+}\big(f_{r}\circ\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}u_{0}\big)(x)~dx\leq\frac{C}{t},

for some constant
C
depending only on

\|u_{0}\|_{{\bf L^{\infty}}},K
, which
yields (
6.2
).
This completes the proof of (ii) in
the case where
S^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}
satisfies
the conditions
stated in Theorem
4.11
.

Step 4.

(proof of (ii) when
S^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}
satisfies the conditions of
Theorem
4.3

or of Theorem
4.9
).

Since
S^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}
satisfies the Oleǐnik-type inequalities (
4.10
) of
Theorem
4.3

(or (
4.34
), (
4.40
) of Theorem
4.9
)
,
with the same analysis of Step 2
we deduce the uniform bound in (
6.2
) for bounded subset
K
of

]-\infty,\mathsf{L}]
or of
[\mathsf{R},+\infty[
.

Next
, for sets
K\subset[0,\mathsf{R}]

or
K\subset[\mathsf{L},0]
, relying on the
Oleǐnik-type inequalities (
4.11
), (
4.12
), of
Theorem
4.3

(or (
4.35
), (
4.41
) of Theorem
4.9
)
,
we recover the bound in (
6.2
) performing
the
same analysis of Step 3.
This completes the proof of (ii) in
the case where
S^{[AB]+}_{t}u_{0}
satisfies
the conditions
stated in Theorem
4.3

or in Theorem
4.9
,
and concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Appendix A
Stability of solutions with respect to connections and BV bounds

We provide here a proof of Properties (iv)-(v) of Theorem
2.8
,
which seems to be absent in the literature. To this end
we first recall a by now classical technical lemma, useful for the analysis
of stability of discontinuous conservation laws (e.g. see
[
17
]
,
[
29
, Proposition 1]
).
For sake of completeness we provide a proof below.

Lemma A.1
.

Fix a connection
(A,B)

and let
I^{AB}
be the map in (
2.10
). Then,
for any couple of pairs
(u_{l},u_{r})
,
(v_{l},v_{r})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
that verify

(A.1)

I^{AB}(u_{l},u_{r})\leq 0,\qquad I^{AB}(v_{l},v_{r})\leq 0\,,

and

(A.2)

f_{l}(u_{l})=f_{r}(u_{r}),\qquad f_{l}(v_{l})=f_{r}(v_{r})\,,

setting

(A.3)

\alpha(u_{l},u_{r},v_{l},v_{r})\doteq\mathrm{sgn}(u_{r}-v_{r})\cdot(f_{r}(u_{r})-f_{r}(v_{r}))-\mathrm{sgn}(u_{l}-v_{l})\cdot(f_{l}(u_{l})-f_{l}(v_{l}))\,,

it holds true

(A.4)

\alpha(u_{l},u_{r},v_{l},v_{r})\leq 0\,.

Proof.

Observe that, if
u_{r}=v_{r}
or
u_{l}=v_{l}
, then the left hand side of (
A.4
) is zero and (
A.4
)
is verified.
Hence,
without loss of generality, we may assume that
u_{r}>v_{r}

and
u_{l}\neq v_{l}
. If
u_{l}>v_{l}
, the left hand side of (
A.4
) is again zero, because of (
A.2
). Thus, assuming that
u_{l}<v_{l}
, we have

(A.5)

\alpha(u_{l},u_{r},v_{l},v_{r})=2(f_{r}(u_{r})-f_{r}(v_{r}))\,.

If we suppose, by contradiction,
that (
A.4
) is not verified,
it would follow by (
A.5
), that

f_{r}(u_{r})>f_{r}(v_{r})
.
Moreover, because of assumptions (
A.1
)-(
A.2
), and applying Lemma
2.5
,
we know that
f_{r}(u_{r}),f_{r}(v_{r})\geq f_{r}(B)
.
Since
u_{r}>v_{r}
, these inequalities together imply that
u_{r}\geq B
.
On the other hand, by (
A.2
), it also holds
f_{l}(u_{l})>f_{l}(v_{l})
.
Relying again on
(
A.1
)-(
A.2
) and Lemma-
2.5
,
we deduce that

f_{l}(u_{l}),f_{l}(v_{l})\geq f_{l}(A)
, which,
coupled with
u_{l}<v_{l}
,

f_{l}(u_{l})>f_{l}(v_{l})
, implies

u_{l}\leq A
. Hence, by Lemma
2.5
it follows that
u_{r}=B
and
u_{l}=A
, and then we would have

(A.6)

\displaystyle\alpha(u_{l},u_{r},v_{l},v_{r})

\displaystyle=\alpha(A,B,v_{l},v_{r})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{sgn}(B-v_{r})\cdot(f_{r}(B)-f_{r}(v_{r}))-\mathrm{sgn}(A-v_{l})\cdot(f_{l}(A)-f_{l}(v_{l}))

\displaystyle=I^{AB}(v_{l},v_{r})\leq 0

which
is a contradiction. Therefore (
A.4
) is satisfied, and the proof is concluded.
∎

In order to obtain stability with respect to perturbations of the connection,
the following quantitative version of Lemma
A.1
will be useful. A general version of this Lemma can be found in
[
16
, Proposition 3.21]
(see also
[
15
, Proposition 2.10]
for the case
f_{l}=f_{r}
).

Lemma A.2
.

Let
(A,B)
,
(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})

be two connections.
Then, for any couple of pairs
(u_{l},u_{r})
,
(v_{l},v_{r})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
that verify

(A.7)

I^{AB}(u_{l},u_{r})\leq 0,\qquad I^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}(v_{l},v_{r})\leq 0\,,

and (
A.2
),
it holds true

(A.8)

\alpha(u_{l},u_{r},v_{l},v_{r})\leq 2\left|f_{r}(B^{\prime})-f_{r}(B)\right|.

Proof.

With no loss of generality assume that
B^{\prime}>B
.
Then, applying Lemma
2.5
, one deduces that
B^{\prime}>B
, together with (
A.2
)
and

I^{A^{\prime}B^{\prime}}(v_{l},v_{r})\leq 0
,
implies that
one of the following two holds:

(1)

I^{AB}(v_{l},v_{r})\leq 0
,

(2)

(v_{l},v_{r})=(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
.

If (1) holds, then by Lemma
A.1
we have
\alpha(u_{l},u_{r},v_{l},v_{r})\leq 0
,
and therefore (
A.8
)
is verified. Otherwise, (2) holds. In this case, we can add and subtract the non positive quantity
I^{AB}(u_{l},u_{r})
,
and rewrite
\alpha
as

(A.9)

\alpha(u_{l},u_{r},A^{\prime},B^{\prime})=\alpha_{r}(u_{r})-\alpha_{l}(u_{l})+I^{AB}(u_{l},u_{r})\leq\alpha_{r}(u_{r})-\alpha_{l}(u_{l}),

where

\displaystyle\alpha_{r}(u_{r})

\displaystyle\doteq\mathrm{sgn}(u_{r}-B^{\prime})\cdot(f_{r}(u_{r})-f_{r}(B^{\prime}))-\mathrm{sgn}(u_{r}-B)\cdot(f_{r}(u_{r})-f_{r}(B)),

\displaystyle\alpha_{l}(u_{l})

\displaystyle\doteq\mathrm{sgn}(u_{l}-A^{\prime})\cdot(f_{l}(u_{l})-f_{l}(A^{\prime}))-\mathrm{sgn}(u_{l}-A)\cdot(f_{l}(u_{l})-f_{l}(A)).

We provide separately an estimate on
\alpha_{r}(u_{r})
and on
\alpha_{l}(u_{l})
. We consider first the term
\alpha_{r}
, and we distinguish three cases.

(1)

u_{r}>B^{\prime}
. Then one has

\alpha_{r}(u_{r})=f_{r}(u_{r})-f_{r}(B^{\prime})-f_{r}(u_{r})+f_{r}(B)=f_{r}(B)-f_{r}(B^{\prime}).

(2)

u_{r}\in[B,B^{\prime}]
. Observe that, applying Lemma
2.5

and relying on (
A.2
) and

I^{AB}(u_{l},u_{r})\leq 0
,
we deduce

f_{r}(u_{r})\geq f_{r}(B)
.
Then one has

\displaystyle\alpha_{r}(u_{r})

\displaystyle=-f_{r}(u_{r})+f_{r}(B^{\prime})-f_{r}(u_{r})+f_{r}(B)

\displaystyle=(f_{r}(B)+f_{r}(B^{\prime})-2f_{r}(u_{r}))\leq(f_{r}(B^{\prime})-f_{r}(B)).

(3)

u_{r}<B
. Then one has

\alpha_{r}(u_{r})=-f_{r}(u_{r})+f_{r}(B^{\prime})+f_{r}(u_{r})-f_{r}(B)\leq f_{r}(B^{\prime})-f_{r}(B).

In every case, we obtain

(A.10)

\alpha_{r}(u_{r})\leq\left|f_{r}(B^{\prime})-f_{r}(B)\right|.

Analogously, and thanks to (
A.2
), we can prove that

(A.11)

\alpha_{l}(u_{l})\geq-\left|f_{l}(A^{\prime})-f_{l}(A)\right|=-\left|f_{r}(B^{\prime})-f_{r}(B)\right|

which in turn, together with (
A.10
), implies

(A.12)

\alpha(u_{l},u_{r},A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\leq 2\left|f_{r}(B^{\prime})-f_{r}(B)\right|,

and this concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.8
-
(iv)
-
(v)
.

Set

(A.13)

u(x,t)\doteq\mathcal{S}_{t}^{\,[AB]+}u_{0}(x),\qquad v(x,t)\doteq\mathcal{S}_{t}^{[A^{\prime}B^{\prime}]+}u_{0}(x).

Relying on property (2)
of Definition
2.2
,
with standard doubling of variable arguments (e.g. see
[
18
, §6.3]
)
one obtains that, for every
non-negative test function
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}
with compact support
contained in
]-\infty,0[\,\times\,]0,+\infty[
, it holds true

(A.14)

\int_{-\infty}^{0}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big\{|u-v|\phi_{t}+\mathrm{sgn}(u-v)\left(f_{l}(u)-f_{l}(v)\right)\phi_{x}\big\}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\geq 0,

and, for for every
non-negative test function
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}
with compact support
contained in

]0,+\infty[\,\times

]0,+\infty[
, it holds true

(A.15)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big\{|u-v|\phi_{t}+\mathrm{sgn}(u-v)\left(f_{r}(u)-f_{r}(v)\right)\phi_{x}\big\}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\geq 0.

Hence, with the same arguments, one deduces that, for every
non-negative test function
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}
with compact support
contained in

\mathbb{R}\times

]0,+\infty[
, it holds true

(A.16)

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\big\{\left|u-v\right|\phi_{t}+\mathrm{sgn}(u-v)(f(x,u)-f(x,v))\phi_{x}\big\}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\geq-E\,,

where
E
is the extra boundary term
at
x=0
(due to the fact that,
differently from (
A.14
)-(
A.15
),
\phi
will not vanish in general at
x=0
)
given by

E=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\big[\mathrm{sgn}(u(x,t)-v(x,t))(f(x,u(x,t))-f(x,v(x,t)))\big]_{x=0-}^{x=0+}\,\phi(0,t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\,,

with
[\cdot]_{x=0-}^{x=0+}
denoting the limit from the right minus the limit from the left at
x=0
.
Observe that,
letting
u_{l},u_{r}
denote the one-sided limit of
u
in
x=0

as in (
2.7
),
and denoting

v_{l},v_{r}
, the corresponding ones
for
v
,
recalling (
A.3
) we can rewrite the quantity
E
as

(A.17)

E=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\alpha(u_{l}(t),u_{r}(t),v_{l}(t),v_{r}(t))\phi(0,t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\,.

On the other hand,
since
u_{l},u_{r}
, and

v_{l},v_{r}

satisfy the Rankine-Hugoniot condition (
2.8
),
together with the inequality
(
2.11
)
related to the
(A,B)
, and

(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
connection, respectively,
applying Lemma
A.2

we deduce that it holds true

(A.18)

\alpha\big(u_{l}(t(,u_{r}(t),v_{l}(t),v_{r}(t)\big)\leq 2\left|f_{r}(B^{\prime})-f_{r}(B)\right|\qquad\text{for a.e. $t>0$\,.}

Thus, combining (
A.16
) with (
A.17
), (
A.8
), we find

(A.19)

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\big\{\left|u-v\right|\phi_{t}+\mathrm{sgn}(u-v)(f(x,u)-f(x,v))\phi_{x}\big\}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\geq-2|f_{r}(B)-f_{r}(B^{\prime})|\int_{0}^{+\infty}\phi(0,t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\,.

Now fix
\tau>\tau_{0}>0
,
R>0
,
and consider the trapezoid

\Omega\doteq\{(x,t)\,:\ \tau_{0}\leq t\leq\tau,\,|x|\leq R+L(\tau-t)\}
,
where
L\doteq\sup_{|z|\leq M}\max\{|f^{\prime}_{l}(z)|,|f^{\prime}_{r}(z)|\}
, with

M
being a uniform
{\bf L^{\infty}}
bound
for
u
and
v
.
Then, by a standard technique
(e.g. see
[
18
, §6.3]
), one can construct a sequence of test functions
\phi_{n}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}_{c}
,
with compact support
contained in

\mathbb{R}\times

]0,+\infty[
, that approximate the characteristic function of
\Omega
when
n\to\infty
.
Employing (
A.19
) with

\phi_{n}
, and letting
n\to\infty
we obtain

(A.20)

\int_{|x|\leq R}\big|u(x,\tau)-v(x,\tau)\big|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{|x|\leq R+L(\tau-\tau_{0})}\big|u(x,\tau_{0})-v(x,\tau_{0})\big|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+2(\tau-\tau_{0})|\big|f_{r}(B)-f_{r}(B^{\prime})\big|\,.

Relying on the
{\bf L^{1}}
-continuity
of
u
and
v
at
\tau_{0}=0

(property (2) of Definition
2.2
),
and letting
R\to\infty

in (
A.20
),
we obtain the estimate of Theorem
2.8
-
(iv)
for
t=\tau
.

To establish property (v) of Theorem
2.8

observe that, if
(A,B)
is a non critical connection, then by Lemma
6.1
-(i)
one has

\mathcal{S}^{AB}_{t}u_{0}\in BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
for all
t>0
, and for any
u_{0}\in{\bf L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Therefore, in this case, relying on this property
we immediately recover the
{\bf L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-Lipschitz
continuity of
t\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{AB}_{t}u_{0}

by standard arguments (e.g. see
[
18
, proof of Theorem 9.4]
).

On the other hand
, in the case of
a critical connection
(A,B)
,
we derive the
{\bf L}^{1}_{loc}
-Lipschitz
continuity of
t\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{AB}_{t}u_{0}

applying Lemma
6.1
-(ii)
and following
the same arguments in
[
25
, proof of Theorem 4.3.1]
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
2.11
.

Relying on Theorem
2.8
-
(iv)
we deduce that

(A.21)

u_{n}(\cdot,t)\quad\ \rightarrow\quad u(\cdot,t)\qquad\text{in}\quad\mathbf{L}^{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{loc}}}(\mathbb{R})\qquad\forall~t\geq 0\,,

which in turn implies that there exists
\overline{x}>0
such that

(A.22)

f_{r}(u_{n}(\overline{x},\cdot))\quad\ \rightarrow\quad f_{r}(u(\overline{x},\cdot))\qquad\text{in}\quad\mathbf{L}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}([0,+\infty[)\,.

Then, observe that by Definition
2.2

u_{n}
and
u
are entropy weak solutions
of
u_{t}+f_{r}(u)_{x}=0
on

]0,+\infty[\,\times\,[0,+\infty[\,
.
Hence, by a general property of weak solutions
(e.g. see
[
18
, Remark 4.2]
),
for every fixed
s>0

one has

(A.23)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}f_{r}(u_{r}(s))ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}f_{r}(u(\overline{x},\cdot))ds+\int_{0}^{\overline{x}}u(z,T)dz-\int_{0}^{\overline{x}}u(z,0)(z)dz\,,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{s}f_{r}(u_{n,r}(s))ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}f_{r}(u_{n}(\overline{x},\cdot))ds+\int_{0}^{\overline{x}}u_{n}(z,T)dz-\int_{0}^{\overline{x}}u_{n}(z,0)dz\qquad\forall~n\,.

Since we are assuming that

\{u_{n}(\cdot,0)\}_{n}
converges to
u(\cdot,0)
in
{\bf L}^{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathrm{loc}}}
,
taking the limit as
n\to\infty
in (
A.23
)
we deduce from (
A.21
), (
A.22
), (
A.23
)
by standard arguments that

(A.24)

f_{r}(u_{n,r})\ \ \rightharpoonup\ \ f_{r}(u_{r})\quad\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}weakly} in}\quad\mathbf{L}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{+}).

With entirely similar arguments one derives also the other convergence in (
2.16
).
∎

Appendix B
Preclusion of
rarefactions emanating from the interface

A distinctive feature of the structure of
AB
-entropy solutions is the fact that
no rarefaction wave can emerge at positive times from the interface
x=0
.
This property was established in
[
2
]

exploiting an
explicit representation formula for
AB
-entropy solutions
a la Lax-Oleǐnik.
A different, rather technical proof, based on a detailed analysis of the structure of
AB
-entropy solutions was derived in
[
6
]
, under the additional assumption that the traces
of the solution at
x=0

admit one sided limits.
Here,
we provide
a much simpler proof that establishes this fact
in the case
of a non critical connection

(A,B)
,
and for a
BV_{\mathsf{loc}}

AB
-entropy solution.
The proof relies on the properties of solutions of Riemann problems and on a blow-up argument.
Namely, the key point is to show that Riemann-type initial data from which rarefaction waves emerge are not attainable by an
AB
-entropy solution at any positive time
t>0
.
Next,
by contradiction and performing a blow-up analysis, we prove that if
a rarefaction emerges from an
AB
-entropy solution at some time
\overline{t}>0
, then there exists a Riemann-type
datum
\overline{u}
that generates a rarefaction
and which is attainable by
an
AB
-entropy solution at time
\overline{t}
.

One can recover this property of preclusion of rarefactions
emanating from the interface
(for any
AB
-entropy solution and general connections)
as a byproduct of the characterization
of attainable profiles

\omega\in\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)
provided by Theorems
4.3
,
4.9
,
4.11
,
4.14

(see Remark
B.4
).

Definition B.1
.

We say that
an
AB
-entropy solution
u(x,t)
to (
1.1
) has a
rarefaction fan emerging at the right (at the left)
from the interface

x=0
at time
\bar{t}
, if
there exists

\delta>0
and two continuity points
0<x_{1}<x_{2}
for
u(\cdot,\overline{t}+\delta)
such that

x_{1}-\delta f_{r}^{\prime}(u(x_{1},\overline{t}+\delta))=x_{2}-\delta f_{r}^{\prime}(u(x_{2},\overline{t}+\delta))=0.

Notice that Definition B.1 does no require to know that the solution
u
admits one-sided limits at
x=0
, and it
is invariant with respect to the scaling

(x,t)\to(\rho x,\overline{t}+\rho(t-\overline{t}))
,
\rho>0
. This definition is equivalent to say that there exists an outgoing rarefaction fan emerging at time
\overline{t}
, at the right, if there exist two distinct genuine characteristics located in
\{x>0\}
for times
t\in\,]\overline{t},\overline{t}+\delta]
,
\delta>0
,
that emerge from the point
(0,\overline{t})
.

Proposition B.2
.

Let
(A,B)
be a connection,
consider a Riemann data

(B.1)

\overline{u}=\begin{cases}u^{-},&x<0,\\
u^{+},&x>0\,,\end{cases}

and assume that
the solution
\mathcal{S}^{[AB]+}_{t}\overline{u}(x)
contains a rarefaction wave
located in the left halfplane
\{x\leq 0\}
,
or in the right one

\{x\geq 0\}
.
Then for every
T>0
it holds
\overline{u}\notin\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)
.

Proof.

By contradiction, suppose that
\mathcal{S}^{[AB]+}_{t}\overline{u}(x)
contains a rarefaction wave
located in
\{x\geq 0\}
,
and assume that

\overline{u}\in\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(T)
, i.e. that there exists an

AB
-entropy solution solution
u(x,t)
of (
1.1
),(
1.2
), such that
u(\cdot\,,T)=\overline{u}
.
Then, by uniqueness, one has

u(x,T+t)=\mathcal{S}^{[AB]+}_{t}\overline{u}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
t\geq 0
.
Since
\mathcal{S}^{[AB]+}_{t}\overline{u}(x)

is a solution of a Riemann problem
containing a rarefaction
with nonnegative characteristic speeds, and because of the admissibility conditions (
2.13
),
it then follows that the right trace
u_{r}
of
u
at
x=0
satisfies

B\leq u_{r}(t)<u(T,x)=u^{+}
for every
t>T
,
x>0
. Tracing the backward characteristics from points
(T,x)
,
x>0
, we find that
u_{r}(t)=u^{+}>B
for every
t\in\,]0,T[
. Therefore,
because of the admissibility conditions (
2.13
),
one has
u_{l}(t)=\pi^{r}_{l,+}(u^{+})

(with
\pi^{r}_{l,+}
defined as in (
4.1
)),
for every
t\in\,]0,T[
.
Then, letting
\xi_{-},\xi_{+}
denote the minimal
and maximal backward characteristics starting
at
(0,T)
, we deduce that
f^{\prime}_{l}(u^{-})=\dot{\xi}_{-}(T)>\dot{\xi}_{+}(T)=f^{\prime}_{l}(\pi^{l}_{r,+}(u^{+}))
,
which in turn implies
u^{-}>\pi^{r}_{l,+}(u^{+})
,
\pi^{l}_{r,+}(u^{-})>u^{+}
. Observe now that the
AB
-entropy solution of a Riemann problem with
initial data (
B.1
) satisfying
u^{-}>\pi^{l}_{r,+}(u^{+})
, and
u^{+}>B
, consists of a single shock
located in the halfplane
\{x\geq 0\}
,
and connecting the left state
\pi^{l}_{r,+}(u^{-})
with the right state
u^{+}
. This is in contrast with the assumption made on
\mathcal{S}^{[AB]+}_{t}\overline{u}(x)
, thus completing the proof.

∎

Proposition B.3
.

Let
(A,B)
be a
non critical
connection,
and let
u

be an
AB
-entropy solution to (
1.1
)
that satisfies
u(\cdot,t)\in BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
, for all
t>0
.
Then
u
does not contain rarefaction waves emerging from the interface
x=0
at times
\overline{t}>0
.

Proof.

Assume by contradiction that the solution
u
has a rarefaction wave,
say located in
\{x\geq 0\}
, which emerges from the interface at some time
\overline{t}>0
.
Let
0<\overline{\rho}<\overline{t}/3
, and
for any
\rho>0
, set

(B.2)

I_{\rho}\doteq\{x\in\mathbb{R}\,:\,|x|\leq\rho\}\,.

Observe that the domain of dependence
of
u(x,t)
, for
(x,t)\in I_{\overline{\rho}}\times[\overline{t}-\overline{\rho},\overline{t}+\overline{\rho}]
, is the trapezoid
\Omega\doteq\{(x,t)\,:\,|x|\leq\overline{\rho}+\Lambda\cdot(\overline{t}+\overline{\rho}-t),\,t\in[\overline{t}-2\overline{\rho},\overline{t}+\overline{\rho}]\}
, where
\Lambda\doteq\sup_{|z|\leq M}\max\{|f^{\prime}_{l}(z)|,|f^{\prime}_{r}(z)|\}
, with

M
being a uniform
{\bf L^{\infty}}
bound
for
u
. Therefore, since
the total variation of
u(t,\cdot)
on
I_{l_{t}}
,

l_{t}\doteq|x|\leq\overline{\rho}+\Lambda\cdot(\overline{t}+\overline{\rho}-t)
, is bounded, and because
(A,B)

is a
non critical
connection, we can invoke the
uniform BV bounds on
AB
-entropy solutions
established in
[
29
, Lemma 8]

(see also

[
1
, Theorem 2.13-(iii)]
)
to deduce that

(B.3)

\mathrm{Tot.Var.}(u(\cdot,t),\,I_{\overline{\rho}})\leq\overline{C}\big(M+\mathrm{Tot.Var.}(u(\cdot,\overline{t}-2\overline{\rho}),I_{(1+2\Lambda)\overline{\rho}})\big)\qquad\forall~t\in\overline{t}+I_{\overline{\rho}}\,,

for some constant
\overline{C}>0
.
Next, consider the
blow-up of
u
at the point
(0,\overline{t}\,)
:

(B.4)

u_{\rho}(x,t)\doteq u(\rho x,\overline{t}+\rho(t-\overline{t}))\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},\ t\geq 0\,,

with
0<\rho<\overline{\rho}/\overline{t}
,
and observe that
it holds true

(B.5)

\mathrm{Tot.Var.}(u_{\rho}(\cdot,t),I_{\overline{\rho}/\rho})\leq\sup_{\tau\,\in\,\overline{t}+I_{\overline{\rho}}}\mathrm{Tot.Var.}(u(\cdot,\tau),I_{\overline{\rho}})\qquad\forall~0\leq t<\overline{t}+\frac{\overline{\rho}}{\rho}\,.

Combining (
B.3
), (
B.5
), we find
a uniform bound on the total variation of

u_{r}(\cdot,t)
on the
interval
I_{\overline{\rho}/\rho}
, for all
t<\overline{t}+{\overline{\rho}}/{\rho}
,
and
0<\rho<\overline{\rho}/\overline{t}
.
Moreover,
observe that
because of the finite
speed of propagation
\Lambda
,
by standard arguments
(e.g. see
[
18
, §7.4]
)
one deduces that

(B.6)

\|u_{\rho}(\cdot,t)-u_{\rho}(\cdot,s)\|_{{\bf L^{1}}(I_{\overline{\rho}/\rho})}\leq\overline{\Lambda}\cdot(t-s)\qquad\forall~0\leq s<t<\overline{t}+\frac{\overline{\rho}}{\rho}\,,

for all
0<\rho<\overline{\rho}/\overline{t}
, and for some constant
\overline{\Lambda}
.
Notice that the sets

I_{\overline{\rho}/\rho}\times[0,\overline{t}+\overline{\rho}/\rho[

invade
\mathbb{R}\times[0,+\infty[
as
\rho\to 0
.
Therefore we can apply
Helly’s compactness theorem
[
18
, Theorem 2.4]
to the sequence
\{u_{\rho}\}_{0<\rho<\overline{\rho}/\overline{t}}
,
and deduce the existence
of a function

v\in{\bf L^{\infty}}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,+\infty[)
,
so that, up to a subsequence,

u_{\rho}(\cdot,t)

converges to
v(\cdot,t)
in
{\bf L^{1}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, as
\rho\to 0
, for all
t>0
.
By Definition
2.2

it follows that
also
v
is an
AB
-entropy solution
of (
1.1
)-(
1.2
), with
u_{0}\doteq v(\cdot,0)
.
Notice that

(B.7)

\lim_{\rho\to 0}u_{\rho}(x,\overline{t}\,)=\overline{u}(x)\doteq\begin{cases}u(0+,\overline{t}\,)\ \ &\text{if}\quad x>0\,,\\
\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\cr u(0-,\overline{t}\,)\ \ &\text{if}\quad x<0\,,\end{cases}

and thus we find

v(\cdot,\overline{t}\,)=\overline{u}\,,

which implies

(B.8)

\overline{u}\in\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(\,\overline{t})\,.

On the other hand, observe that the rarefaction wave which emerges in the solution
u
at time
\overline{t}
is preserved by the blow-ups

u_{\rho}
in (
B.4
), because
it is self similar for the scaling
(x,t)\mapsto(\rho x,\bar{t}+\rho(t-\bar{t}))
. Therefore there will be a rarefaction wave emerging at time
\overline{t}
, and located in
\{x\geq 0\}
, also in the solution
v
. This in turn implies
that the solution
\mathcal{S}_{t}^{AB}\overline{u}(x)
to the Riemann problem with initial datum
\overline{u}

contains a rarefaction emerging at

t=0
and located in
\{x\geq 0\}
, since
\mathcal{S}_{t}^{AB}\overline{u}(x)=v(\bar{t}+t,x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
t\geq 0
.
This, together with (
B.8
),
is in contradiction with Proposition
B.2
, thus completing the proof.
∎

Remark B.4
.

In the case of a general connection
(A,B)
,
relying on the characterization of

\mathcal{A}^{[AB]}(t)
,
t>0
,
provided by Theorems
4.3
,
4.9
,
4.11
,
4.14
,
we can show that
no rarefaction can emerge from the interface
x=0
at any time
\overline{t}>0

for any
AB
-entropy solution
u
to (
1.1
),
as follows.
Suppose, by contradiciton, that a rarefaction is generated in
a time interval
[\,\overline{t},\overline{t}+\delta]
, for some
\delta>0
,
and that lies in the semiplane
\{x\geq 0\}
.
In particular this means that there exist
two genuine characteristics
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}:[\,\bar{t},\bar{t}+\delta\,]\to\ [0,+\infty[\,
, such that

\xi_{1}(\,\bar{t}\,)=\xi_{2}(\,\bar{t}\,)=0
,

\overline{x}_{1}\doteq\xi_{1}(\,\bar{t}+\delta)<\overline{x}_{2}\doteq\xi_{2}(\,\bar{t}+\delta)
.
We may also assume that
\xi^{\prime}_{i}=f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(\overline{x}_{i}))
,
i=1,2
.
Let
\omega\doteq u(\cdot,\overline{t}+\delta)
,
\mathsf{R}\doteq\mathsf{R}[\omega,f_{r}]
(see def (
4.2
)), and consider the time
\tau(x)=(\overline{t}+\delta)-x/f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(x))
,
x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[\,
, at which the characteristic starting at
(x,\overline{t}+\delta)
, with slope
f^{\prime}_{r}(\omega(x))

impacts the interface
x=0
.
Notice that
\tau(\overline{x}_{1})=\tau(\overline{x}_{2})=\overline{t}
.
Moreover, thanks to Lemma 4.4 in
[
6
]
,
the Oleǐnik estimates satisfied by
\omega
(because of condition (i) or (i)’ of Theorems
4.3
,
4.9
,
4.11
,
4.14
) imply the strict monotonicity of the map
x\to\tau(x)
,
x\in\,]0,\mathsf{R}[\,
. In turn, the strict monotonicity of
\tau
implies

\tau(\overline{x}_{1})\neq\tau(\overline{x}_{2})
, thus contradicting the assumption

\tau(\overline{x}_{1})=\tau(\overline{x}_{2})=\overline{t}
.

Appendix C
Semicontinuity properties of solutions to
convex conservation laws

Solutions to conservation laws with
convex flux enjoy a lower and upper semicontinuity
property
with respect to the
{\bf L}^{1}

convergence
as stated in the following

Lemma C.1
.

Given a uniformly convex map
f
,
and
T>0
, let

\{u_{n}\}_{n}
be
a sequence of
entropy weak solutions
of

(C.1)

u_{t}+f(u)_{x}=0\quad\ x>0,\quad t\in[0,T],

that admit a
strong trace
u_{n}(0+,t)=\lim_{x\to 0+}u_{n}(x,t)

at
x=0
,
for all
t\in[0,T]
,
and let
u
be
an
entropy weak solution
of (
C.1
)
that admits a
strong trace

u(0+,t)=\lim_{x\to 0+}u(x,t)

at
x=0
,
for all
t\in[0,T]
.
Assume that
\{u_{n}\}_{n}
are uniformly bounded
in
{\bf L}^{\infty}
, that

(C.2)

u_{n}(\cdot,t)\ \ \rightarrow\ \ u(\cdot,t)\qquad\text{in}\quad\mathbf{L}^{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{l}oc}}(\,]0,+\infty[),\qquad\forall~t\in[0,T]\,,

and that

(C.3)

f(u_{n}(0+,\cdot))\ \ \rightharpoonup\ \ f(u(0+,\cdot))\qquad\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}weakly} \ in}\quad\mathbf{L}^{1}([0,T])\,.

Then,
for every
x\geq 0
,
it holds true

(C.4)

\displaystyle u(x+,T)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\
y\to x,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y>0}\end{subarray}}\;u_{n}(y+,T)\,.

If we assume that
u_{n},u
, are entropy weak solutions of
u_{t}+f^{\prime}(u)_{x}=0
on
x<0,\,t\in[0,T]
, and that
the convergences (
C.2
), (
C.3
),
hold in
\mathbf{L}^{1}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathsf{l}oc}}(\,]-\infty,0[)
,
and for the left traces in
x=0
, respectively, then
for every
x\leq 0
, it holds true

(C.5)

\displaystyle u(x-,T)

\displaystyle\geq\limsup_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\
y\to x,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y<0}\end{subarray}}\;u_{n}(y-,T)\,.

Proof.

We will establish only the inequality (
C.4
), the proof of (
C.5
) being entirely similar.
Given
x\geq 0
,
T>0
, consider a sequence
\{y_{n}\}_{n}
,

y_{n}>0
, converging to
x
, and such that

(C.6)

\lim_{n}u_{n}(y_{n}+,T)=\liminf_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\to\infty\\
y\to x\end{subarray}}\;u_{n}(y+,T)\,.

Let
\vartheta_{n}^{+}:\ ]\tau_{n},T]\to\ ]0,+\infty[\,
,
\tau_{n}\geq 0
,
denote the maximal backward characteristic
for
u_{n}
starting from
(y_{n},T)
,
with the property that either
\tau_{n}=0
, or

\lim_{t\to\tau_{n}}\vartheta_{n}^{+}(t)=0
.
By possibly taking a subsequence, we can assume that either
\tau_{n}=0
for all
n
, or that

\lim_{t\to\tau_{n}}\vartheta_{n}^{+}(t)=0
for all
n
.
We recall that a maximal backward characteristic
for
u_{n}

passing through
(y_{n},T)
,
y>0
,
is a genuine (shock free)
characteristics
whose trajectory
is a segment with constant slope
f^{\prime}(u_{n}(y_{n}+,T))

(e.g. see
[
25
]
).
Notice that
\{\vartheta_{n}^{+}\}_{n}
is a sequence of
Lipschitz continuous functions with a uniform Lipschitz constant

\sup_{|u|\leq M}f^{\prime}(u)

(
M
being a uniform
{\bf L}^{\infty}
bound on
u_{n}
), defined on uniformly bounded intervals
]\tau_{n},T]
.
Hence, by Ascoli-Arzelà Theorem we can assume that, up to a subsequence,
\{\vartheta_{n}^{+}\}_{n}
converges uniformly to some Lipschitz continuous function
\vartheta:\ ]\tau,T]\to\ ]0,+\infty[\,
, such that

(C.7)

\displaystyle\tau=0,\qquad\text{if}\qquad\tau_{n}=0\quad\forall~n\,,

\displaystyle\tau=\lim_{n\to\infty}\tau_{n}\,,\quad\lim_{t\to\tau}\vartheta(t)=0,\qquad\text{if}\quad\lim_{t\to\tau_{n}}\vartheta_{n}^{+}(t)=0\quad\forall~n\,,

and such that
\vartheta(T)=x
.
By a general property of
characteristics, the uniform limit of genuine characteristics is also a genuine characteristic.
This can be easily verified
in this context
observing that the trajectory of
a genuine characteristic passing through a point
(y,t)
,
y>0
, is
a segment connecting
(y,t)
with the point

(0,\tau(y,t))
or
with the point
(z(y,t),0)
, where
\tau(y,t)
and
z(y,t)

denotes the points of minimum for the functionals involved in
the Lax-Oleǐnik
representation formula of solutions
for the boundary value problem (see
[
35
]
), and that such functionals are
{\bf L}^{1}
continuous with respect to the initial datum and
and weakly continuous in
{\bf L}^{1}
with respect to the flux-trace of the solution at
x=0
.
Therefore it follows that

\vartheta
is a genuine characteristic with constant slope
\vartheta^{\prime}

satisfying

(C.8)

\vartheta^{\prime}=\lim_{n\to\infty}(\vartheta^{+}_{n})^{\prime}=\lim_{n\to\infty}f^{\prime}(u_{n}(y_{n}+,T)),\qquad\qquad\vartheta^{\prime}\geq f^{\prime}(u(x+,T))\,.

Since
f^{\prime}
is increasing,
we deduce from (
C.8
) that

(C.9)

\lim_{n}u_{n}(y_{n}+,T)\geq u(x+,T)\,,

which, together with (
C.6
), yields (
C.4
).

∎
